# X — 100 DOCUMENTED POSTS — 2026-11-02 BIG PULL (101 dossiers delivered)

Built for Garrett's decision: every post fully documented (what it is, where to find it, how to use it, where it plugs into his systems, creative angles, risks, first test + verdict). Nothing was filtered out by usefulness — Garrett decides what to use.

**Sources:** the X For You sweep's inventoried-only backlog (posts inventoried in the Oct 30 – Nov 2 sweeps but never dossiered), watchlist-priority (@sfdata9ers, @PattonAnalytics, @boxscorescout, @ghatz_28, @HaydenWinks, @nfl_simulator, @ProGridSports, @KoalatyStats, @SumerSports, @run_the_sims, @The_Coach_A, @StatsHubCom, @Timo_NFL, @GridironInfo_, @ihartitz). Fresh feed / fresh watchlist passes were not needed — the backlog alone exceeded quota.

**Count: 101 dossiers** (100 requested; batch 8 completed its set with one reserve after a swap, so one extra is included rather than discarded). Verdict mix: TEST 14 · LEARN-ONLY 34 · CONTEXT-ONLY 51 · WATCH 2. The CONTEXT-ONLY weight is honest: much of the backlog is live-game reaction fragments, each cataloged with what little it carries and what the account is good for.

## THE DOSSIERS

### #1 — @run_the_sims — 2116829431879127447
Post: https://x.com/run_the_sims/status/2116829431879127447
TEXT RECOVERY: PARTIAL — post not present in the 2026-11-01-am / 2026-11-01-pm raw per-account files (checked run_the_sims.txt/.json in all three named subdirs); dossier works from the inventoried transcription: "QB Pressure vs EPA chart: Allen most pressured yet +0.05 EPA under pressure (transcribed)."

1. WHAT IT IS — A two-axis QB chart: x = pressure rate faced (pressured dropbacks ÷ total dropbacks), y = EPA per dropback *while pressured*. EPA (Expected Points Added) = EP(after play) − EP(before play), where EP is the expected net points of the possession given down, distance, yard line, and time/score state, from the standard nflfastR/nflverse EP model. The transcribed headline cell: Josh Allen (BUF) sits at the highest pressure rate on the chart while still posting +0.05 EPA/dropback under pressure — i.e., he is the only archetype that is both the most-attacked and still net-positive when the pocket collapses. All other chart coordinates are UNVERIFIED / image-locked. Underlying data is almost certainly charted pressure data (PFF / FTN / NGS class) joined to play-by-play EPA — pressure is NOT in public play-by-play, so the pressure flag itself comes from a charting/tracking vendor, not from nflverse.
2. WHERE TO FIND IT — Post: https://x.com/run_the_sims/status/2116829431879127447 . Primary data sources: nflverse (EPA side) https://github.com/nflverse ; SumerSports' QB stats explorer, which exposes a `pressured` refinement and defines EPA/play the same way: https://sumersports.com/players/quarterback/?refinement=pressured&plays=100
3. HOW TO USE IT — GSE can compute the EPA half today: pull nflverse play-by-play via `nflreadpy` (Python) / `nflreadr::load_pbp()` (R), filter dropbacks, group by passer, mean EPA. The pressure half needs a pressure flag: (i) FTN charting distributed through nflverse (2022+, includes blitz/pressure-adjacent charting, CC-BY-SA), (ii) NGS passing data (pressure/time-to-throw aggregates, public weekly files), or (iii) PFF (paid, license-restricted). Approximation without a vendor: use sack + QB-hit + hurry proxies from PFR advanced passing — coarser, must be labeled a proxy, never "pressure rate."
4. WHERE TO USE IT — GSE prediction engine, **matchups layer** (QB-vs-pass-rush matchup) feeding **situations**. It extends the engine's existing QB rating with a pressure-resilience split: a QB who holds EPA under pressure discounts an opposing elite pass rush; a QB who craters under pressure upgrades it. It replaces the single blended QB EPA number in matchup reasoning with a clean/pressured pair — exactly the split the Reasoning Doctrine requires to be visible in the published trail.
5. CREATIVE ANGLES — (a) Pair with the Timo_NFL OL/QB pressure-decomposition dossier in this same batch (1672229769541238784): Timo separates who *causes* pressure; this chart measures who *survives* it — together they make a full pressure matchup: pressure created × EPA survived. (b) Content OS: "most pressured, still positive" is a one-chart Sunday-morning tease that gives away no pick. (c) Props: pressured-EPA collapse is a live trigger for QB under / interception reasoning when an OL injury hits mid-week (health layer cross-feed).
6. RISKS AND LIMITS — Pressure definition varies by vendor (hurry thresholds differ PFF vs FTN vs NGS) — cross-vendor numbers are not interchangeable. Pressured dropbacks are a selected sample (QBs who extend plays self-select into pressure). Small denominators early in a season. The +0.05 figure is a transcription from a prior sweep, UNVERIFIED against the image in this pass.
7. FIRST TEST AND VERDICT — Smallest test: rebuild the chart from nflverse EPA + FTN pressure charting for Weeks 1–8 2026 and check whether Allen is (a) top pressure rate and (b) ≈ +0.05 pressured EPA within ±0.03. Verdict: **LEARN-ONLY** (TEST after the rebuild matches).

### #2 — @GridironInfo_ — 2117264667923886282
Post: https://x.com/GridironInfo_/status/2117264667923886282
TEXT RECOVERY: PARTIAL — full recovered text from raw file (2026-11-02-am/GridironInfo_.txt, Post 6, Mon 02 Nov 2026 14:36:48 GMT): "Week 8 Penalty Yards". All values are in the chart image: UNVERIFIED / image-locked.

1. WHAT IT IS — A single-week (Week 8, 2026) team ranking of total penalty yards: sum of yards enforced against each team in its Week 8 game (accepted penalties; declined/offsetting fouls contribute 0 enforced yards in the standard box-score convention). It is a raw discipline/luck hybrid — penalty *yards* weights 15-yard fouls (face mask, roughing, DPI spot fouls) far above 5-yard procedural fouls, so the chart mixes pre-snap discipline with judgment-call variance. No formula beyond aggregation; underlying data is the official game book / play-by-play penalty records.
2. WHERE TO FIND IT — Post: https://x.com/GridironInfo_/status/2117264667923886282 . Primary source for the same quantity: Pro Football Reference team game logs / drive and penalty data at https://www.pro-football-reference.com/ , and nflverse play-by-play (penalty flags and yards on every play) at https://github.com/nflverse .
3. HOW TO USE IT — Fully computable by GSE from data it can legally get free: nflverse PBP has `penalty`, `penalty_team`, `penalty_yards` fields — `load_pbp(2026) |> filter(week==8, penalty==1) |> group_by(penalty_team) |> sum(penalty_yards)`. Split it before using it: procedural (false start, delay, illegal formation — stable, coach/QB-driven) vs judgment (DPI, holding, roughing — high variance, partly crew-driven, cf. the run_the_sims "Luck + Penalties" WP-swing work already in the corpus).
4. WHERE TO USE IT — GSE prediction engine, **venues-weather-refs layer** (crew assignment × team procedural-penalty tendency) with a secondary feed to **situations**. A single Week-8 chart replaces nothing; as a rolling 4-week procedural-penalty rate it extends the refs layer from "which crew" to "which crew × which team's foul profile."
5. CREATIVE ANGLES — (a) The DPI-spot-foul component is a hidden totals input: two DPI-prone secondaries + a flag-happy crew is a field-position and scoring accelerant no box score shows. (b) Content OS: weekly "most/least disciplined" is evergreen, pick-free content. (c) Pair with the corpus's luck+penalties WP work to separate "undisciplined" (repeatable) from "unlucky with whistles" (regression candidate) — opposite betting implications from the same chart.
6. RISKS AND LIMITS — One week, ~120–140 team-penalties league-wide: noise dominates. Penalty yards ≠ penalty count ≠ WP impact. DPI yardage depends on where fouls occur (spot fouls). Image values UNVERIFIED. GridironInfo_ states no methodology or source on the post.
7. FIRST TEST AND VERDICT — Test: reproduce the Week 8 ranking from nflverse in one query and spot-check the top and bottom team against PFR game books. Verdict: **CONTEXT-ONLY** (the rolling procedural split, not this one-week chart, is the TEST candidate).

### #3 — @ihartitz — 2117256504353206421
Post: https://x.com/ihartitz/status/2117256504353206421
TEXT RECOVERY: PARTIAL — full recovered text from raw file (2026-11-02-am/ihartitz.txt, Post 8, Mon 02 Nov 2026 14:04:22 GMT): "Jaguars ... might be the best team in the NFL?" Chart values UNVERIFIED / image-locked. Corroboration recovered in the same raw pull: @SumerSports RT @SyedSchemes (02 Nov 2026): "One team is in the top 5 in EPA/play on offense and EPA/play allowed on defense, per @SumerSports: the Jacksonville Jaguars."

1. WHAT IT IS — An EPA scatter making a "complete team" claim for Jacksonville: offensive EPA/play (points added per play vs expectation, nflfastR-class EP model) on one axis, defensive EPA/play allowed on the other. The claim's precise form, corroborated same-day by SumerSports: JAX is the *only* team top-5 in both offensive EPA/play and defensive EPA/play allowed at that point in 2026. That is a balance claim, not a "best raw offense" claim — a team can sit in that quadrant while being 4th–5th in each rather than 1st in either. 1. WHAT IT IS — Week 8 team time of possession (TOP): total game-clock time each team's offense held the ball in its Week 8 game, in minutes:seconds — sum of drive durations. TOP is an *outcome*, not an efficiency metric: it is driven by (a) number of drives, (b) run/pass mix and completion rate (incompletions stop the clock), (c) pace/plays per drive, and (d) game script (leading teams possess differently). Two teams can post identical TOP with opposite offensive quality.
2. WHERE TO FIND IT — Post: https://x.com/GridironInfo_/status/2117282647407788174 . Primary sources: official box scores / Pro Football Reference game pages, https://www.pro-football-reference.com/ ; drive-level reconstruction from nflverse PBP (drive start/end clock) at https://github.com/nflverse .
3. HOW TO USE IT — GSE can compute it from nflverse PBP drive fields, but the usable derivatives are what matter: seconds per play (pace), plays per drive, and TOP *split by game state* (leading/trailing/neutral script). Raw single-game TOP should never enter a model directly. All inputs are free in nflverse/PFR.
4. WHERE TO USE IT — GSE prediction engine, **situations layer** only, as a pace/script descriptor feeding total-plays reasoning (more plays = more variance and more counting-stat volume for props), and **usage layer** indirectly (drive volume = opportunity volume). It extends the pace inputs; it replaces no efficiency input, and must never substitute for EPA in the matchups layer.
5. CREATIVE ANGLES — (a) TOP extremes flag clock-control matchups: a high-TOP rushing team vs a thin defense is a 4th-quarter fatigue story the reasoning can cite with the defense's snap counts attached. (b) Combine with average drive start (this batch): long TOP + bad starting position = a team that moves slowly *and* starts deep — a specific under-mechanism. (c) Content OS: TOP is one of the most fan-recognized stats; a "who actually controlled Week 8" post is safe evergreen content.
6. RISKS AND LIMITS — Single game; opponent- and script-driven; overtime inflates; TOP correlates weakly with winning once efficiency is accounted for. Image values UNVERIFIED. Treating TOP as quality is the classic box-score fallacy this dossier exists to prevent.
7. FIRST TEST AND VERDICT — Test: none warranted for the chart itself; if pace derivatives are wanted, validate seconds/play from PBP against PFR drive times for one Week 8 game. Verdict: **CONTEXT-ONLY**.

1. WHAT IT IS — Week 8 average drive start position: for each team, the mean starting yard line of its offensive drives in Week 8, conventionally expressed as yards from its own goal (own 25 = 25) or as yards-to-go (75). It is a *composite* stat with three distinct parents: (a) defense (turnovers and stops create short fields), (b) special teams (punt/kick coverage and returns), (c) opponent punting. It is therefore not an offensive stat at all — it measures the field position a team's other two units hand its offense. Its value is mechanical: expected points of a drive rise monotonically with starting position, so drive start converts directly into hidden points via the EP curve.
2. WHERE TO FIND IT — Post: https://x.com/GridironInfo_/status/2117273584296984770 . Primary sources: Pro Football Reference drive data (average start per team/game), https://www.pro-football-reference.com/ ; reproducible from nflverse PBP — first play of each drive carries the yard line — https://github.com/nflverse .
3. HOW TO USE IT — GSE computes it exactly: group PBP by game + drive, take the first play's `yardline_100`, average (as yards-to-go, then flip for display), excluding end-of-half kneel drives with the exclusion stated. Then monetize it: multiply a team's drive-start edge (vs league mean) by drives/game and by the EP slope (~0.05–0.06 EP per yard in midfield regions, from the nflfastR EP model) to get hidden points per game — a number the reasoning can publish. Inputs: nflverse only, free, CC-BY 4.0.
4. WHERE TO USE IT — GSE prediction engine, **everything-else layer** (special teams / field-position composite) with a cross-feed to **matchups** (a defense that manufactures short fields is worth points the EPA-allowed column understates, because EPA-allowed is partly *caused* by long fields). As a rolling multi-week stat it extends the engine's team ratings with a field-position adjustment; the one-week chart itself replaces nothing.
5. CREATIVE ANGLES — (a) The mirror stat — opponent drive start — is a clean special-teams-unit grade: punt team + coverage in one number. (b) Drive-start edge is a totals input nobody prices directly: two teams with big positive drive-start edges play on a shorter field all day. (c) Content OS: "hidden yards" explainer content — show fans the points a punt unit is worth, pick-free.
6. RISKS AND LIMITS — Week-8 single game: 10–13 drives per team, one takeaway swings the mean by ~4 yards. Turnover luck is the largest component and regresses. Chart does not state whether kickoff-only or all drives, or the kneel-down treatment — methodology UNVERIFIED, values image-locked.
7. FIRST TEST AND VERDICT — Test: compute Weeks 1–8 rolling drive start from nflverse, convert to EP/game, and check correlation with next-4-week scoring vs raw EPA alone. Verdict: **LEARN-ONLY** (rolling version is a TEST candidate for the everything-else layer).

PPD is the drive-level sibling of EPA/play: it captures finishing (TD vs FG vs empty) that per-play EPA smooths over, and it is the unit bookmakers' totals ultimately cash on.

Rolling 4–6 week PPD, split offense/defense-allowed, is a legitimate totals input; single-week PPD is a recap stat. All inputs free.

### #11 — @Timo_NFL — 2117264539376841150
Post: https://x.com/Timo_NFL/status/2117264539376841150
TEXT RECOVERY: FULL (reply explainer, no chart) — from raw file (2026-11-02-am/Timo_NFL.txt, Post 5, Mon 02 Nov 2026 14:36:17 GMT): "He only started moving post-snap. It's basically like a receiver who isn't lined up at the line of scrimmage. Happens on every snap, just not that extreme. Still not allowed to move forward pre-snap"

1. WHAT IT IS — A rules explainer about pre-snap movement, answering (from context, a clip of a player appearing to move early) why no foul was called. The governing NFL framework (2026 Official Playing Rules, Rule 7, Section 4): after any shift, all 11 offensive players must come to a simultaneous complete stop for one full second; at the snap, at most one player may be in motion, and that motion must be parallel to or away from the line of scrimmage — no player may be moving *forward* (toward the opponent's goal) at or before the snap. Timo's two distinctions are exactly the operative ones: (a) movement that begins *after* the snap is not a pre-snap foul at all, however early it looks on video — timing is judged against the snap, not against the other 21 players; (b) a backfield player not on the line has more legal pre-snap latitude (like a receiver off the line), but the forward-motion prohibition is absolute for everyone pre-snap. Violations split into false start (dead ball, simulates the snap / abrupt forward movement by a set player) vs illegal shift/motion (live-ball, 5 yards) depending on the act.
2. WHERE TO FIND IT — Post: https://x.com/Timo_NFL/status/2117264539376841150 . Primary source: the NFL rulebook provisions Rule 3-18/3-30 (definitions) and Rule 7-4-2, 7-4-6–8 (set/shift/motion), summarized with the 2026 rulebook as controlling source at https://sportrules.org/american-football/illegal-formation-illegal-shift-and-illegal-motion-rules/
3. HOW TO USE IT — No computation: this is adjudication knowledge. The usable form for GSE is a checklist applied to "was that a missed false start?" moments: (1) did movement start before the snap? (2) was the player set for 1s after the last shift? (3) was any pre-snap motion forward? If any public content or reasoning ever cites a "missed call," it must pass this checklist with the rule cited — otherwise it does not publish. It also sharpens film-reading of *legal* pre-snap movement: jet/orbit motion that stays lateral is a designed tell (defense must declare coverage rotation), which is a scheme input, not a rules issue.
4. WHERE TO USE IT — GSE **Content OS** (rules-literacy content and reply accuracy — the Reply Subject Check failure mode is answering the wrong question; rules replies must be rule-cited) and the research corpus. It touches the prediction engine only at the **venues-weather-refs layer** margin: crews differ in false-start/shift enforcement strictness, and this is the rule text any crew-tendency claim must reference. Replaces nothing; it is reference knowledge that prevents a class of factual errors.
5. CREATIVE ANGLES — (a) A standing "legal or not?" content format — clip, checklist, rule cite — is high-engagement, pick-free, and builds the account's credibility with exactly the audience that buys analysis. (b) Motion-legality knowledge inverts into scheme scouting: teams that live at the edge of the 1-second set rule generate cheap defensive encroachment/false-start asymmetry worth charting per team. (c) Agent fleet: this is a model fact-check template — a claim, a primary rule text, a verdict — reusable for any rules-adjacent claim in any lane.

### #13 — @Timo_NFL — 1672229769541238784
Post: https://x.com/Timo_NFL/status/1672229769541238784
TEXT RECOVERY: FULL — recovered verbatim from the deep-math raw pull (2026-10-30-deepmath/w_Timo_NFL.json, original post Fri 23 Jun 2023): "I built a model which separates OL & QB priors for pressure & sacks. Using that, I can simulate sack/pressure % against a generic D — Fields — w/ CHI OL: 43% pressure, 11.4% sack — w/ PHI OL: 33% pressure, 10.5% sack — We've often said: pressure belongs (more) to OL, sacks belong to QB"

1. WHAT IT IS — A variance-decomposition model for pass protection. Construction as stated: estimate *separate priors* for the offensive line (unit-level pressure propensity) and the QB (his own pressure/sack propensity), then simulate a given QB behind a given OL against a generic (league-average) defense to get counterfactual pressure% and sack%. Pressure% = dropbacks with QB pressure ÷ dropbacks; sack% = sacks ÷ dropbacks. The worked example (Justin Fields, 2023 context): swapping Chicago's OL for Philadelphia's moves pressure 43% → 33% (−10 pts, the OL's share) but sacks only 11.4% → 10.5% (−0.9 pts) — empirically demonstrating the model's thesis: **pressure creation/avoidance is mostly an OL (and scheme/defense) property, while sack conversion is mostly a QB property** (time to throw, pocket movement, throwaway decisions). The statistical form (hierarchical/Bayesian priors over QB and OL units fit on historical dropbacks, then posterior predictive simulation) is inferred from "priors" + "simulate" — Timo published no paper or repo with this post, so the exact estimator is UNVERIFIED; the estimand and the example numbers are stated verbatim.
2. WHERE TO FIND IT — Post: https://x.com/Timo_NFL/status/1672229769541238784 . Primary source: NONE public — no repo, paper, or methodology page is linked in the post or was found attached to it; the post itself is the citable artifact. Input data for any rebuild: nflverse PBP (sacks are in PBP; pressure requires FTN charting via nflverse or NGS/PFF) at https://github.com/nflverse .
3. HOW TO USE IT — GSE can build its own version from legally available data: (i) response variables per dropback — sacked (in PBP) and pressured (FTN charting in nflverse, 2022+; NGS pressure aggregates as cross-check); (ii) fit a hierarchical logistic model (e.g., Stan/brms or PyMC) with varying intercepts for QB, OL unit (team-season, or starting-five grouping when snap data is joined), and defensive team; (iii) simulate counterfactuals by swapping the OL intercept for a fixed QB against a league-average defense intercept — exactly Timo's Fields exercise, for any QB/OL pair. Priors shrink small-sample QBs toward the mean, which is the honest way to handle backup-QB spot starts. This is a known-feasible build with GSE's stack (NUTS/hierarchical Bayes already in the adopted research stack).
4. WHERE TO USE IT — GSE prediction engine, **matchups layer** as a named sub-model (pass-protection matchup) feeding **health** (an OL injury = swap the OL prior for the backup-unit prior and re-simulate, instead of a hand-waved "OL downgrade") and QB-change spots (new QB behind same OL: pressure expectation holds, sack expectation moves with the QB prior). It replaces the engine's blended team sack-rate input with an attributed pair — and per the Reasoning Doctrine, the published reasoning can then say "the pressure is the line's, the sacks are the QB's" with numbers, which is precisely the mechanism-level reasoning GSE sells. Research corpus: files next to the run_the_sims pressure/EPA chart in this batch — the two are complements (creation/attribution × survival).
5. CREATIVE ANGLES — (a) Trade/deadline and free-agency valuation: a QB's sack problem that follows the *QB* prior is not fixed by signing linemen — a front-office content series and an offseason research product. (b) Draft: college pressure-to-sack conversion is one of the stickier QB translation stats — the same decomposition on college data grades QB prospects on the trait that survives. (c) Live/DFS: when a starting QB is a late scratch, the model reprices the backup's sack exposure behind *that* OL in minutes — a concrete props edge workflow (sack props, QB unders) with the mechanism stated. (d) Combine with the elusiveness dossier: QB sack-avoidance prior × RB/WR YAC traits = a full "creation after breakdown" profile for broken-play EPA.
6. RISKS AND LIMITS — 2023 post; no public code, no validation published, estimator UNVERIFIED — GSE must treat its rebuild as a new model, not a replication. OL "unit" is not stable within a season (injuries rotate starters; team-season intercepts smear that). Pressure labels are charting-vendor property with the usual cross-vendor disagreement. Scheme (play-action rate, dropback depth, quick-game share) confounds both priors — a QB in a quick-game offense posts a flattering sack prior that may not travel. Counterfactuals extrapolate: Fields-behind-PHI was never observed, and the simulation assumes additivity (no QB×OL interaction).
7. FIRST TEST AND VERDICT — Smallest real test: fit the sack half only (fully identified from free PBP, no charting needed) on 2022–2025, QB + team-season OL varying intercepts; check out-of-sample (2026 Weeks 1–8) whether the decomposed model beats raw team sack rate at predicting team-game sacks. If it wins, add the FTN pressure half. Verdict: **TEST**.

1. WHAT IT IS — A sign-direction correction, not a stat. At 15:47 GMT the account posted a link-only chart post (https://x.com/boxscorescout/status/2116920187496902695, t.co link only in the raw pull); seven minutes later this post clarifies the chart's defensive stats run higher = worse; eight minutes after that the account posted "yea im an idiot 🤦‍♂️" (2116923927406760137). The mechanism: defensive counting/efficiency stats are almost always "allowed" stats — yards allowed, points allowed, EPA allowed per play (opponent EPA on plays against that defense), success rate allowed. For every one of them a larger number means the defense performed worse, the exact inverse of the offensive version of the same stat. A chart that plots defensive stats on the same visual logic as offensive stats (longer bar / higher rank = better) silently inverts its own ranking unless the axis, ordering, or labeling is flipped. Which specific defensive stats were in the chart is UNVERIFIED / image-locked — the chart itself was not readable in any pull.
2. WHERE TO FIND IT — Post: https://x.com/boxscorescout/status/2116921725594349848. Primary source for the chart: NONE recoverable (t.co link target not resolved; chart values image-locked). Primary source for computing the underlying class of stats yourself: nflverse play-by-play releases, https://github.com/nflverse/nflverse-data — defensive EPA/play allowed, success rate allowed, and points per drive allowed are all derivable from that play-by-play.
3. HOW TO USE IT — There is nothing to install; the usable artifact is a chart-QA rule. Any GSE defensive chart must state its direction in the graphic itself ("higher = worse" footer, or invert the scale so better defenses always plot "up"). Computation path GSE already has: pull a season of nflverse play-by-play, filter to plays where the team in question is on defense, aggregate EPA and success rate allowed per play/drive — no new data source, license, or cost.
4. WHERE TO USE IT — GSE Content OS (every published defensive chart, matchup card, and 360° card) and the GSE prediction engine's matchups layer display output. It replaces nothing; it extends the chart pipeline with a sign-convention check that prevents a whole class of reader-inverting errors — the exact error this creator caught in himself in public, in real time.
5. CREATIVE ANGLES — (a) A one-line "direction linter" in the card-generation code: any stat whose name contains "allowed" must carry a direction flag before the card renders. (b) Turn the correction itself into content format: GSE publishing its own corrections visibly and fast (7 minutes here) is a trust signal competitors rarely match. (c) Combine with the PattonAnalytics legend lesson elsewhere in this batch ("Left number is sacks, right number is scrambles") — a standing GSE chart standard: direction + legend + units on every graphic, no exceptions.
6. RISKS AND LIMITS — The underlying chart is unreadable, so no value from it can be cited, ranked, or reused — UNVERIFIED / image-locked. The creator's own follow-up ("yea im an idiot") suggests the original chart may itself have been mislabeled, not just misread, so even the clarification may not fully describe what was plotted. One-line social corrections also travel less far than the original chart, so the misreading likely persisted for part of the audience.
7. FIRST TEST AND VERDICT — Smallest test: apply the direction-footer + inverted-ordering check to the next GSE defensive matchup chart before it ships and confirm a cold reader can state which defense is better in five seconds. Verdict: CONTEXT-ONLY for the post's content (no reusable data), with the chart-direction QA rule adopted as a process note for the Content OS.

1. WHAT IT IS — A live in-game anomaly observation from Timo Riske (PFF Senior Data Scientist, per PFF's analyst roster). The metric is team net passing yards, defined precisely as gross passing yards (sum of yards gained on completions) minus yards lost on sacks — the box-score "net yards passing" line. At the moment of posting, Tampa Bay's net passing total was -2 yards: their completions had gained less than their sacks had surrendered. Yet Tampa Bay led by 16 points — the game finished Atlanta 0, Tampa Bay 16 (independently corroborated: a Week 8 recap describes Tampa Bay beating Atlanta "without completing a single pass" in the scoring sense the recap intends, Penix Jr.'s worst game, 16-0 final). Mechanism: win probability in a low-possession, defense/field-position game can be carried entirely by defense, special teams, rushing, and opponent failure; passing efficiency — normally the strongest single driver of winning — can be net-negative on a winning team for a full game when the opponent's offense is even worse and the leading team has no incentive to accept passing variance.
2. WHERE TO FIND IT — Post: https://x.com/Timo_NFL/status/2116985107211039035. Creator verification: https://www.pff.com/analysts (Timo Riske, Senior Data Scientist). Score corroboration: https://www.gridiron-magazine.com/nfl/tgs-2026-week-8-broncos-take-control (Week 8 scores list: Atlanta Falcons 0 @ 16 Tampa Bay Buccaneers). HOW TO USE IT — GSE can compute net passing yards for any team-game from play-by-play in one aggregation: sum pass yards on completions, subtract sack yards lost (sack plays are flagged in nflverse play-by-play with yardage). A historical scan — every team-game since 1999 with net passing yards ≤ 0, and their win rate / scoring margin — is a one-query research-corpus exercise that converts a tweet-sized curiosity into a measured base rate. It replaces nothing; it extends the engine's sense of how extreme a non-passing win can be, which matters for live/derivative reasoning and for not over-updating on a QB's box score from a game his team won on defense.

CREATIVE ANGLES — (a) Build the anomaly detector generally: a weekly automated scan for statistical absurdities (negative net passing in a win, 100+ rushing yards in a shutout loss, etc.) from play-by-play, feeding the Content OS with verified oddities instead of vibes. ### #24 — @Timo_NFL — 2117033531931262989
Post: https://x.com/Timo_NFL/status/2117033531931262989
Full text recovered: "He had like double digit steps and almost 10 yards after the catch, how can you call that incomplete on the field." (Sun, 01 Nov 2026 23:18:21 GMT, raw file 2026-11-01-pm/Timo_NFL.txt). TEXT RECOVERY: FULL.

1. WHAT IT IS — A catch-rule application dispute. Timo's claim, in plain terms: a receiver secured the ball, took double-digit steps, and gained almost 10 yards after the catch (YAC), yet the play was ruled incomplete on the field. Measured against the actual rule — NFL Rulebook Rule 8, Section 1, Article 3, as summarized in the Eagles' Football 101 explainer of the official rulebook — a catch requires three elements in order: (a) secure control of the ball before it touches the ground; (b) both feet (or another body part other than hands) down in bounds; (c) an act common to the game (tucking the ball, turning upfield, taking an additional step, extending the ball) OR maintaining control long enough to perform one. Ten-plus steps and ~10 yards of advance after securing the ball satisfies element (c) many times over under any reading — at that point the receiver is a runner, and a subsequent loss of the ball is a fumble, not an incompletion. So the post is an officiating-consistency complaint with a precise rules basis, not a vibes complaint. The specific play, player, and game are NOT named in the post — that attribution is UNVERIFIED, and Timo's step/YAC counts are his eyeball estimates, UNVERIFIED against tracking data.
2. WHERE TO FIND IT — Post: https://x.com/Timo_NFL/status/2117033531931262989. Primary source for the rule: NFL Rulebook Rule 8, Section 1, Article 3, via https://www.philadelphiaeagles.com/news/football-101-making-a-catch-13912991 (team-published summary of the official rulebook text). A second explainer applying the same three elements to a contested call: https://www.profootballnetwork.com/what-is-a-catch-in-the-nfl-a-look-at-the-leagues-rule-and-why-its-so-controversial/.
3. HOW TO USE IT — GSE cannot adjudicate catches from standard play-by-play (the play is simply recorded as complete/incomplete), but the elements are approximable with tracking data where available: NGS-class tracking gives catch point, steps/time between secure control and ball dislodged, and YAC before contact — enough to flag "process-complete catches ruled incomplete" systematically rather than anecdotally. Without tracking, the honest GSE use is rules literacy: content and reasoning that cites the three elements by number instead of arguing from slow-motion feeling.
4. WHERE TO USE IT — GSE prediction engine, venues-weather-refs layer: officiating variance (catch/no-catch, replay overturn tendencies) is a real, if small, situational factor, and a log of disputed process calls is the raw material for measuring it instead of assuming it. GSE Content OS: a "what the rule actually says" explainer is evergreen, tease-safe content that fits the reasoning-first brand.
5. CREATIVE ANGLES — (a) A disputed-call log, kept weekly with rule citations, becomes a proprietary officiating-variance dataset over a season — nobody else in GSE's content tier is building one. (b) Pair with replay rules: on-field ruling vs. "clear and obvious" replay standard explains most fan confusion — the same play can be a catch by the book and stand as incomplete after review, and explaining that gap is differentiated content. (c) Player-prop angle: receivers whose YAC comes on contested/process catches carry hidden incompletion variance that box-score props ignore.
6. RISKS AND LIMITS — The play is unnamed, so this dossier documents a rules point, not an event; do not attach it to any specific Week 8 play without independent verification. Eyeball step counts ("like double digit") are estimates. The third element's "long enough to perform an act" clause retains official discretion, so even a by-the-book argument is an argument, not a proof, for any specific call.
7. FIRST TEST AND VERDICT — No engine test; the first action is clerical — start the disputed-call log with this post as entry zero (rule cited, play unattributed). Verdict: CONTEXT-ONLY.

### #25 — @Timo_NFL — 2117022958132883835
Post: https://x.com/Timo_NFL/status/2117022958132883835
Full text recovered: "Can't blame KOC, he wanted to go for it but then he saw the twitter thread about how nobody is factoring in uncertainty!" (Sun, 01 Nov 2026 22:36:20 GMT, raw file 2026-11-01-pm/Timo_NFL.txt). TEXT RECOVERY: FULL.

1. WHAT IT IS — A joke with a real paper inside it. "KOC" is Kevin O'Connell, Minnesota Vikings head coach. Timo's gag: O'Connell wanted to go for it on a fourth down, then saw "the twitter thread about how nobody is factoring in uncertainty" and backed off. The thread being referenced is the public discussion of the fourth-down uncertainty-quantification literature, whose primary source is Brill, Yurko & Wyner, "Analytics, have some humility: a statistical view of fourth-down decision making" (arXiv:2311.03490, verified at source for this dossier). The paper's mechanism, precisely: the standard procedure picks the fourth-down option (Go / FG / Punt) that maximizes a win-probability (WP) point estimate from a machine-learning model fit on historical play-by-play (1999–2022, via nflFastR; primary fitting window 2006–2021, 600,825 plays, 4,101 non-tied games). Those WP models are, in the authors' words, "overfit high-variance estimators, exacerbated by the highly correlated nature of football play-by-play data" — every play in a game shares one win/loss outcome, so effective sample size is far smaller than play counts suggest. Bootstrapping the WP model and propagating that uncertainty through the decision shows the estimated optimal decision is far less certain than broadcast graphics imply: in their worked example, a play where a popular model calls Go a +3.6% WP "strong" decision becomes, under their procedure, a +1.4% point gain with a 90% confidence interval of [-3.9%, +5.1%] and 50% of bootstrapped models preferring the field goal — a coin flip wearing a recommendation's clothes. Their rule: recommend a decision only when the uncertainty interval supports confidence that it beats all alternatives; for a substantial share of fourth downs, the honest output is "too close to call."
2. WHERE TO FIND IT — Post: https://x.com/Timo_NFL/status/2117022958132883835. Primary source: https://arxiv.org/html/2311.03490v4 (Brill, Yurko & Wyner). Replication code, named in the paper itself: https://github.com/snoopryan123/fourth_down. Background explainer of the same limitation (point estimates without intervals, variance preferences for favorites/underdogs): https://lukeneuendorf.substack.com/p/building-a-college-football-4th-down.
3. HOW TO USE IT — Directly implementable in GSE's stack: (a) take any WP/EP model GSE already runs on nflverse play-by-play; (b) cluster-bootstrap it by game (resample games, not plays — the paper's whole point is within-game correlation); (c) for any decision-grade output, report the point estimate, the interval, and the bootstrap share preferring each option; (d) adopt the emission rule: publish a confident recommendation only when the interval clears the alternatives, otherwise publish "no confident edge" — which is an abstention standard GSE's verdict system already understands. Inputs: historical play-by-play (nflverse, free), the existing model, and compute for a few hundred bootstrap refits — feasible on Kaggle-tier compute. The paper's code is R/nflFastR-based; a Python port against nflreadpy is straightforward.
4. WHERE TO USE IT — GSE prediction engine, situations layer (fourth-down and late-game decision context), and — more importantly — the engine's reasoning/emission layer globally: this is the formal version of Garrett's standing doctrine that the reasoning, with what it weighed and discounted, is the product. A pick or a coach-grade published with a WP point estimate and no interval is precisely the failure this paper documents. It extends every WP-based output GSE ships and replaces false precision with a stated confidence tier; it also sharpens the "accountability" discourse in the Timo dossier above — both coaches AND analysts get graded against uncertainty, not outcomes or point estimates.
5. CREATIVE ANGLES — (a) Confidence-tiered publishing: label GSE outputs by interval width (clear edge / lean / no confident edge) — a visible honesty feature no picks competitor in GSE's tier offers, and a natural paywall divider (full intervals and bootstrap shares inside the pack, tier label outside). (b) Apply the same cluster-bootstrap discipline outside fourth downs: prop projections, totals, and SGP legs all inherit correlated-play variance — the method transfers to the entire emission gate, which is the second-order use the fleet should not miss. (c) Content: "the analytics bot said go and it was still a coin flip" is a ready explainer that positions GSE against both anti-analytics and blind-analytics camps.
6. RISKS AND LIMITS — The paper's data ends in 2022 and its headline examples use specific public WP models (Baldwin's, Burke's); magnitudes will differ for GSE's own model and must be re-measured, not imported. Cluster bootstrapping a large WP model is compute-real, not free. Uncertainty quantification also cuts both ways, as Timo's joke itself notes: "too close to call" can become an all-purpose excuse for the conservatism the original models correctly identified — the paper's finding is that fewer decisions are obvious, not that going for it is usually wrong. The joke's target (a specific O'Connell decision) is not identified in the post and is UNVERIFIED here.
7. FIRST TEST AND VERDICT — Smallest real test: pick one Week 9 fourth-down spot where a public model recommends Go, refit GSE's WP model on 200 game-cluster bootstrap resamples from nflverse play-by-play, and check whether the Go-vs-kick interval includes zero. One spot, one evening, binary learning: either GSE's point estimates are sturdier than the paper's examples or they are not. Verdict: TEST — the method is peer-style, code-available, and aimed at GSE's single most-published number type.

The claim: four teams are simultaneously elite-defense and bad-dropback-offense — the classic "win ugly / under" team profile. Tampa Bay is the one recovered member.

Primary source: SumerSports team EPA data (their published splits include EPA/play and EPA/dropback — see the per-player/team tables at https://sumersports.com/players/fa8e600/jordan-love/ for the format); replication source: nflverse/nflfastR pbp — https://cran.r-project.org/web/packages/nflfastR/readme/README.html .

2. WHERE TO FIND IT — Post URL above. Primary source: SumerSports data platform — https://www.databricks.com:443/customers/sumersports ; replication source: nflverse pbp carries air_yards per attempt, so game-level aDOT is `mean(air_yards)` over pass attempts by game — https://cran.r-project.org/web/packages/nflfastR/readme/README.html .
3. HOW TO USE IT — Computable by GSE from nflreadr in one aggregation: group 2026 pbp by game and posteam, mean air_yards on pass attempts, sum the two teams, rank all games ascending, and verify KC@DEN sits last — recovering the exact truncated number from data. For engine use, the durable form is team-level: each offense's aDOT trend and each defense's aDOT *allowed* (do defenses force short throws, or choose to?), split by coverage shell where charting allows — nflverse gives the attempt side; coverage splits need NGS/PFR-class sources.
4. WHERE TO USE IT — GSE prediction engine, matchups layer (aDOT is a scheme-interaction metric: a low-aDOT offense vs a tackle-well, rally defense is a different matchup than the same offense vs a soft zone) and the props side of matchups (low combined aDOT games compress WR air-yard and longest-reception props, inflate RB/TE target share). Situations adjacency: wind/venue games systematically depress aDOT — cross-check the venues-weather-refs layer before crediting the defenses.

(b) aDOT *divergence* (one team high, one low in the same game) is the more tradeable shape: it predicts which team's script breaks first when trailing. (c) Pair aDOT with yards-after-catch: low-aDOT + high-YAC offenses (designed YAC) are sustainable; low-aDOT + low-YAC is a broken offense — the pair separates scheme from failure.
6. RISKS AND LIMITS — Game script contaminates aDOT (leading teams throw shorter; trailing teams are forced deep or check down under pressure); one game is an extreme-value stat, and extremes are selected *because* they are extreme. ### #40 — @Timo_NFL — 2117252614777618468
Post: https://x.com/Timo_NFL/status/2117252614777618468
Full text recovered from raw sweep file (2026-11-02-am/Timo_NFL.txt): "idk if the Vikings blow it up, it's not even an incompletion but a fumble and return for six. Not sure if that's repeatable in reliable way"
1. WHAT IT IS — A repeatability/regression claim about defensive scoring plays, not a metric with a formula. The mechanism: a fumble recovery returned for a touchdown chains three low-base-rate, high-variance events — a fumble occurring, the defense (rather than the offense, roughly a coin flip on loose balls) recovering it, and the return reaching the end zone. Each link is weakly controlled by the defense; forced fumbles have a modest skill component, recovery and return-for-six are dominated by ball bounce, field position, and pursuit angles. The underlying data source for testing it is play-by-play (nflverse/PFR), where fumble recoveries, return yards, and defensive TDs are play-level fields.
2. WHERE TO FIND IT — Post: https://x.com/Timo_NFL/status/2117252614777618468 — Primary source: https://nflanalytic.com/explainer-turnovers-luck.html (turnover-luck explainer: turnover margin is among the least repeatable team stats season-to-season; fumble recoveries regress toward 50/50; the repeatable inputs underneath are forced fumbles and pressure, not recovery outcomes).
3. HOW TO USE IT — Computable entirely from data GSE can legally get: pull nflverse play-by-play, flag defensive fumble-return TDs, and split any team's scoring into (a) offensive/defensive scrimmage EPA and (b) non-offensive TD points. The repeatable test is split-half / year-over-year correlation of defensive TD rate vs. forced-fumble rate and pressure rate — the literature pattern is that the TD rate barely persists while the forcing inputs persist modestly. No tracking data needed.
4. WHERE TO USE IT — GSE prediction engine, everything-else layer (team-quality / luck adjustment), feeding matchups: when a defense's recent results are propped up by return TDs, its rating gets regressed toward its scrimmage-play EPA before any matchup number is produced. It extends the engine's existing regression discipline; it replaces nothing, but it stops one specific error — pricing a defense at face value off a highlight play.
5. CREATIVE ANGLES — (a) A "borrowed points" field on every team page: points scored off return TDs and short fields, shown next to the record, as paid-pack context. (b) Combine with the Jessie Bates punch-out note elsewhere in this sweep — forced-fumble skill (punch technique) is the repeatable half of this post; a forced-fumble-rate feature is the constructive version of Timo's doubt. (c) Content OS angle: "the least repeatable play in football" is a clean explainer topic that teaches regression without giving away a pick.
6. RISKS AND LIMITS — The post is an opinion ("idk", "not sure"), no data cited — UNVERIFIED as stated, though consistent with the published turnover-luck literature. Single-play sample. Defensive TDs do correlate weakly with pressure and forced fumbles, so the discount should be partial, not total. Return TDs also cluster for genuinely fast defenses in specific game scripts.
7. FIRST TEST AND VERDICT — Smallest test: one nflverse query, 2015–2025, year-over-year correlation of team defensive-TD rate (expect near zero) vs. forced-fumble rate (expect small positive). Verdict: LEARN-ONLY — a correct prior to encode as a regression rule, not a feature to build today.

1. WHAT IT IS — The Mismatch Manifesto is a weekly matchup-chart system built on one idea, stated by the author: "one metric to show a mismatch instead of going 'Offense ranks x, defense ranks y.'" Each chart is a COMBINED rate: the offense's rate and the opposing defense's allowed rate, summed. Documented components: (a) Combined Explosive Play Rate — offense's explosive pass rate + defense's explosive pass rate allowed, and the same for runs (explosive pass defined as 15+ yards in the recent thread editions, 20+ in older PFF editions; explosive run 10+ / 15+ respectively — definitions shifted across editions, so the current edition's thresholds are UNVERIFIED); (b) Combined Pressure Rate — OL pressure rate allowed per dropback + opposing defense's pressure rate generated per dropback (higher = QB in trouble); (c) Yards Before Contact (combined, run game); (d) Combined Pass Yards Per Dropback; (e) EPA (combined). Historical data source: PFF charting; the current (Fantasy Points era) source is not stated in the recovered text.
2. WHERE TO FIND IT — Post: https://x.com/ihartitz/status/2113269088256749571 — Primary source for the method: http://pff.com/news/hartitz-mismatch-manifesto-biggest-fantasy-football-mismatches-in-week-17 (published Manifesto edition with full definitions of combined explosive rate, combined pressure rate, and combined pass yards per dropback). The Week 7 edition itself lives only in the post's images.
3. HOW TO USE IT — Almost everything is computable from data GSE can legally get: nflverse PBP gives play-level yards, so explosive pass/run rates (any threshold), yards before contact is NOT in standard PBP (needs charting — PFF/FTN — or an NGS-style approximation), pass yards per dropback and EPA are native nflverse (EPA ships with nflverse PBP). Pressure rate is the gap: nflverse PBP does not chart pressures; PFR's weekly pressure data or a paid charting source is required, or pressure must be approximated via sack+hurry proxies with the approximation labeled. Build: for each game, for each side, combined_rate = off_rate + def_allowed_rate, rank all 32 offenses per component.
4. WHERE TO USE IT — GSE prediction engine, matchups layer — this is the single most directly transplantable item in this batch: a combined-rate matchup table is precisely the shape a matchup layer wants, and it extends the engine's current offense-vs-defense comparisons by replacing rank-vs-rank narration with summed rates. Secondarily GSE Content OS / 360° spotlight cards, where the chart format itself (one number per matchup per facet) is a proven, readable paid-vault visual.
5. CREATIVE ANGLES — (a) Publishable reasoning trail: under the Reasoning Doctrine, a combined-rate table is an ideal "what we weighed" artifact — each play's reasoning can cite the 2–3 combined rates that drove it. (b) Extend the sum to a z-scored differential and backtest whether combined rates beat rank-differences at predicting team totals — turning a content chart into a validated engine feature. (c) Add the layers Hartitz doesn't: combined rates split by early/late downs and by personnel, using nflverse fields. (d) DFS lane: combined explosive rate is a stacking-environment screen for the Sunday slate.
6. RISKS AND LIMITS — Summing two rates double-counts league average (a sum of two ~10% rates is not itself a rate) — fine for ranking, wrong as a probability. Pressure and YBC components depend on charting GSE does not currently license. Chart values for Week 7 are image-locked and UNVERIFIED.
7. FIRST TEST AND VERDICT — Smallest test: build the EPA + explosive components from nflverse for one week (both are PBP-native), eyeball against the post's chart ordering where legible, and backtest combined explosive rate vs. actual team totals over 2023–2025. Verdict: TEST — method is documented, mostly computable in-house, and aimed at the exact layer GSE runs on.

### #62 — @HaydenWinks — 2117032725849018754
Post: https://x.com/HaydenWinks/status/2117032725849018754
Recovered text (full, raw file): "the 4th down bot decisions in a tab" — a fragment, apparently noting that he keeps the 4th Down Bot's decisions open in a browser tab during games. The bot referenced is the well-known NFL 4th Down Bot (Ben Baldwin / rbsdm); that identification is context inference from the fragment plus the model's public footprint, flagged as inference.
1. WHAT IT IS — The 4th-down decision model: for every 4th-down situation it estimates win probability (WP) under each choice — go, field goal, punt — for an average team, as a function of score differential, time remaining, field position, yards to go, timeouts, and modeled conversion probability and field-goal make probability, then reports the WP gained or lost by the coach's actual decision. Data underneath: nflfastR / nflverse play-by-play. This is the one fully-real, open-source tool in this batch.
2. WHERE TO FIND IT — Post URL above. Primary sources (verified at source this pass): the model package https://github.com/nflverse/nfl4th (nflverse org; the CRAN README at https://rdrr.io/cran/nfl4th/f/README.md states verbatim "This is the package that powers the fourth down calculator" and links the repo), and the live calculator https://rbsdm.com/stats/fourth_calculator/ , whose own notes state the model is built on @nflfastR data and warn against use in overtime and with extreme caution in the final minute.
3. HOW TO USE IT — Install from the docs: R package `nfl4th` on CRAN (author Ben Baldwin), or call its functions on any play-by-play frame with the situation fields (yardline, ydstogo, quarter, clock, score differential, timeouts). Inputs are exactly the fields nflverse play-by-play already carries, so GSE can run it on data it already legally pulls — no scraping, no paid source. The published uncertainty refinement (bootstrap agreement rate on the recommendation) exists in the literature: Brill et al., "Analytics, have some humility" (arXiv:2311.03490), https://arxiv.org/html/2311.03490v4 .
4. WHERE TO USE IT — GSE prediction engine, situations layer: (a) pre-game, as a coaching-aggressiveness feature per head coach (historical WP left on the table by decision); (b) in-game/second-half content, scoring decisions live. It extends the situations layer with a decision-quality signal GSE does not currently compute, at zero data cost. Secondary: GSE Content OS postgame "decision report card" format.
5. CREATIVE ANGLES — Combine per-coach 4th-down WP leakage with matchup data: a coach who systematically punts in +WP go spots is worth expected points to the opponent that no box-score model sees. Pair with the Timo_NFL uncertainty fragment in this batch (2117007753936060632) to publish the bootstrap-humility version — recommendation plus agreement rate — which no major account currently leads with. Also a clean agent-fleet test task: a Jules session can wire nfl4th against the existing nflverse pull in one bounded job.
6. RISKS AND LIMITS — The model prices decisions for an *average* team: it cannot know a specific offense's conversion edge or a kicker's true range, and its own calculator disclaims overtime and final-minute use. Point-estimate recommendations overstate confidence (the arXiv critique: selection bias and score-differential bias in WP models). Hayden's post itself is a fragment — the bot identification is inference, though the tool's existence and mechanics are independently verified above.
7. FIRST TEST AND VERDICT — Smallest real test: run nfl4th over one completed week of nflverse play-by-play, output each head coach's total WP gained/lost on 4th-down decisions, and hand-check three decisions against the public rbsdm calculator. Verdict: TEST (the strongest item in this batch; adoption decision after the one-week run reproduces the calculator's numbers).

1. WHAT IT IS — A consistency-of-offense claim built on EPA/play game percentiles. Definitions: EPA (Expected Points Added) per play = the change in expected points (from a model's expected-points surface over down, distance, and field position) across a play, averaged over an offense's plays in a game. The "75th percentile or higher" framing ranks each Jacksonville game against the distribution of all team-game EPA/play performances — i.e., in 5 of 7 games the Jaguars' offense graded in the top quartile of team-games. The percentile distribution and EPA model here are SumerSports' own (proprietary team analytics data); the claim as recovered is a creator claim, UNVERIFIED against an independent recomputation.
2. WHERE TO FIND IT — Post URL above (repost of @SyedSchemes, data attributed to @SumerSports throughout the same raw capture). Primary source: SumerSports' own analytics platform (sumersports.com — the data vendor named in the capture); no public methodology page for their EPA percentile table was verified in this pass, so the citation Garrett should use is the post itself plus an independent nflverse recomputation (below).
3. HOW TO USE IT — Fully approximable from data GSE can legally get: nflverse play-by-play ships with `epa` per play from the nflfastR expected-points model. Compute team-game offensive EPA/play for every team-game in the season, build the percentile distribution, and count each team's games at/above the 75th percentile — a "top-quartile frequency" consistency feature in a dozen lines of R/Python. Inputs needed: play-by-play with EPA and a play-type filter (drop kneels/spikes by convention — the convention chosen must be documented, since SumerSports' filter is not public).
4. WHERE TO USE IT — GSE prediction engine, matchups layer (offensive consistency / floor feature) with a situations-layer read: a team that hits the top quartile in 5 of 7 games has a higher weekly floor than its mean EPA/play alone shows, which matters for totals and team-total reasoning. It extends the engine's EPA features from level (mean) to distribution (frequency of high-end games) — it replaces nothing, and it must be computed from nflverse, not copied from the post.
5. CREATIVE ANGLES — Pair top-quartile frequency with its mirror (bottom-quartile frequency) to build a two-number "floor/ceiling profile" per offense — a publishable GSE Content OS graphic and a paid-card input in one artifact. Cross with opponent defensive EPA percentiles for a true matchup-of-distributions view instead of mean-vs-mean. The Colts sibling post in the same capture ("1st percentile performance by EPA/play against the Jaguars in Week 8") shows the same table generates content in both directions.
6. RISKS AND LIMITS — Truncated recovery (PARTIAL): the season scope and any qualifier after "this sea…" are unseen. Seven games is a small sample; percentile-of-team-games depends on the EPA model and play-filter conventions, so GSE's recomputation may not reproduce 5-of-7 exactly — a mismatch would be a methods difference, not proof either side is wrong. SumerSports' underlying data is proprietary; only the nflverse approximation is GSE-usable.
7. FIRST TEST AND VERDICT — Smallest real test: the nflverse recomputation in §3 for Jacksonville's first seven games; if it lands at 4–5 of 7 at/above the 75th percentile, the feature is sound and the post is corroborated within methods tolerance. Verdict: TEST (feature); the post's exact count stays UNVERIFIED until that run.

### #65 — @sfdata9ers — 2112260996941414825
Post: https://x.com/sfdata9ers/status/2112260996941414825
TEXT RECOVERY: PARTIAL — no per-account raw file for this ID; full text not recovered. Recovered text (seen-index): "QB EPA/Play vs. Success Rate (Weeks 1 - 6, pre-MNF, min. 90 plays)". All QB names and chart coordinates are image-locked / UNVERIFIED.

1. WHAT IT IS — A two-axis QB efficiency scatter for Weeks 1–6 (pre-MNF), minimum 90 plays. X-axis concept: EPA/play. EPA (Expected Points Added) = EP after the play − EP before the play, where EP is the expected net points of the next score given down, distance, and field position (nflfastR/nflverse EP model). Y-axis concept: success rate — share of plays that succeed. Two definitions circulate and the post does not state which it used: (a) the yardage rule — ≥40% of yards to go on 1st down, ≥60% on 2nd, 100% on 3rd/4th; (b) the nflverse EPA rule — any play with EPA > 0. That ambiguity matters: the two definitions do not rank QBs identically. The pairing is the point: EPA/play measures magnitude and explosiveness (one 40-yard bomb moves it), success rate measures down-to-down consistency/floor (every play counts once). A QB high-EPA / low-SR is boom-bust; low-EPA / high-SR is a chain-mover who creates no explosives. Underlying data source: not stated in the recovered text; this account's other posts in this batch use @FantasyPtsData charting, but attributing that source to this chart is UNVERIFIED.
2. WHERE TO FIND IT — Post: https://x.com/sfdata9ers/status/2112260996941414825 . Primary source for the metrics themselves (open, reproducible): nflverse play-by-play via nflreadr — https://github.com/nflverse/nflreadr — and the success-rate definition reference at https://nflanalytic.com/explainer-success-rate.html .
3. HOW TO USE IT — GSE can compute both axes exactly from data it can legally get free: `nflreadr::load_pbp()` (R) or `nflreadpy` (Python, `pip install nflreadpy`) returns play-by-play with pre-computed `epa` and a success flag per play, 1999–present, updated nightly in season. Filter to QB dropbacks + QB runs (or all plays with that QB's team on offense — the post's "min. 90 plays" denominator is ambiguous between dropbacks and team plays, another reason not to copy its coordinates), group by passer, take mean EPA and mean success. No paid feed is needed for this chart; the only input GSE cannot replicate from nflverse is a vendor's private EP model variant, which changes values at the margins, not the shape.
4. WHERE TO USE IT — GSE prediction engine, **matchups layer** (QB vs. opposing defense), feeding the **usage layer** secondarily. It replaces nothing; it extends the engine's QB rating from a single EPA number to an EPA × consistency pair, which is exactly the distinction that decides whether a QB props line (completions, attempts, longest completion) or a team total is the right market expression. Also GSE Content OS: this scatter is a proven, cheap weekly chart format for public tease content (categories and shape only — no picks/lines in public copy).
5. CREATIVE ANGLES — (a) Flip the axes to the defensive version (EPA/play allowed vs. success rate allowed) and subtract: QB boom-bust profile vs. the specific defense's boom-bust allowance is a matchup interaction neither side shows alone. (b) Track a QB's week-over-week migration across the quadrants as an offensive-line health proxy — SR falls before EPA does when protection breaks. (c) Pair with the Penix pressure dossier in this same batch (2116303527024959750): a high-SR/low-EPA QB whose SR collapses under pressure is a different fade than a boom-bust QB.
6. RISKS AND LIMITS — Six weeks at a 90-play floor is a small, schedule-contaminated sample; EPA/play is opponent- and game-script-sensitive and is not opponent-adjusted by default in nflverse. Success-rate definition unstated (see §1). Pre-MNF snapshot means one Week 6 game is missing entirely. Chart values image-locked / UNVERIFIED — do not quote any QB's coordinates from this post.
7. FIRST TEST AND VERDICT — Smallest real test: rebuild the chart in-house from `load_pbp()` for the same Weeks 1–6 window under both success definitions and check whether the quadrant membership (not the exact coordinates) is stable across definitions. Verdict: **LEARN-ONLY** — the method is standard and already computable in-house; the post itself adds no new data.

1. WHAT IT IS — A coverage-suppression leaderboard built on two charted quantities: coverage snaps (plays where the defender is assigned in coverage on a pass play, from film charting / tracking assignment) and receiving yards allowed as the nearest/assigned defender (yards gained on completions where that corner was the coverage defender of record). The filter (≥150 coverage snaps AND <100 yards allowed) selects for volume-qualified corners who are barely being completed on — the "not targeted, or targeted and clamped" group. The rate form of the same idea is yards per coverage snap (receiving yards allowed ÷ coverage snaps), which SumerSports publishes elsewhere (a secondary report citing SumerSports put one qualifying CB at 1.12 yards per coverage snap, 68th among qualifiers — that figure is about a different player/post and is cited here only to show the metric family, UNVERIFIED for this post). Underlying data: SumerSports' own charting/tracking operation (SūmerSports, https://www.sumersports.com — front-office staff plus a frame-level tracking model, per its own published material). This is charted assignment data, not box-score data: who "allowed" a catch is a charting judgment.
2. WHERE TO FIND IT — Post: https://x.com/SumerSports/status/2112640012227645625 . Primary source: SumerSports — https://www.sumersports.com . The closest free public analogue for coverage-adjacent data is the FTN charting file in nflverse (see dossier 2095240387472273741), which does NOT carry nearest-defender yards allowed.
3. HOW TO USE IT — GSE cannot compute true yards-allowed-per-coverage-snap from nflverse/PFR alone: nflverse play-by-play names the targeted receiver, not the assigned coverage defender. Legal approximations, in descending fidelity: (a) PFR advanced defense tables give per-defender targets, completions, and yards allowed as nearest defender (PFR's own charting, Sports Info Solutions-sourced) — the standard public substitute; (b) NGS via nflverse (`load_nextgen_stats`) gives separation and targeted-defender-adjacent aggregates for some leaderboards but not a full CB yards-allowed table; (c) Fantasy Points Data / PFF are the paid charting routes if GSE ever buys one. Inputs needed for the approximation: PFR advanced-defense per-player targets/yards + snap counts (`load_snap_counts()` in nflreadr, PFR-sourced, free).
4. WHERE TO USE IT — GSE prediction engine, **matchups layer** — specifically the WR-vs-CB leg of receiver props and QB passing lines: a WR1 facing a sub-100-yards-allowed corner on 150+ snaps is a downgrade signal for that WR's yardage prop and an upgrade for the offense's secondary options. It extends the matchup layer, which currently reasons team-level pass defense (EPA allowed) but not individual coverage assignment. Also GSE Content OS / DFS packs: "shadow corner" downgrades are a concrete, explainable DFS ownership lever.
5. CREATIVE ANGLES — (a) Invert it: corners with high coverage snaps and LOW targets are being avoided — avoidance itself is the stat, and it pushes targets to the other side of the field, which is a targets funnel for the WR2/slot in DFS. (b) Combine with the man/zone QB dossier in this batch (2113749051917095414): a clamp corner who only plays zone is a different problem for a WR than one who travels in man. (c) Track the list week to week — a corner falling off the <100-yards list is an injury/form signal days before it shows in team EPA allowed.
6. RISKS AND LIMITS — Assignment charting is judgment-based and vendors disagree on who allowed a catch, especially in zone. The <100-yards threshold rewards corners whom QBs avoid (selection effect) as much as corners who contest well. 150 snaps through the post's window is still a modest sample; one broken coverage (a 60-yard TD on a bust that may not even be his assignment) moves a corner on or off the list. Names/values in this specific post UNVERIFIED (partial recovery).
7. FIRST TEST AND VERDICT — Smallest real test: pull PFR advanced-defense nearest-defender yards + nflverse snap counts for the current season, apply the same ≥150 coverage-snap / <100-yard filter, and see whether the resulting list is plausible against team pass-defense EPA. Verdict: **LEARN-ONLY** — valuable matchup concept, but the exact input is vendor-charted; GSE should run the PFR approximation, not quote Sumer's list.

### #67 — @sfdata9ers — 2113749051917095414
Post: https://x.com/sfdata9ers/status/2113749051917095414
TEXT RECOVERY: PARTIAL — no per-account raw file for this ID; full text not recovered. Recovered text (seen-index, truncated): "Which QBs are coverage-proof - and which have a weakness? EPA/DB vs. Man & Zone Data: @Fan[tas yPtsData, truncated in the index]". The data credit reads as @FantasyPtsData (consistent with this account's other posts in this batch) but the truncation makes that attribution UNVERIFIED for this post. All per-QB man/zone values are image-locked / UNVERIFIED.

1. WHAT IT IS — A coverage-split QB chart: for each QB, EPA per dropback (EPA/DB = total EPA on dropbacks ÷ dropbacks, where a dropback = pass attempt + sack + scramble) computed separately against man coverage and against zone coverage, as charted by the data vendor. The spread between a QB's man EPA/DB and zone EPA/DB is the "weakness" axis: a QB who is elite vs. zone but negative vs. man (or vice versa) is coverage-scheme-sensitive — "coverage-proof" QBs sit near the same EPA/DB against both. The mechanism is real football: man coverage stresses separation-winning and timing/anticipation throws to isolated receivers; zone stresses read speed, void-finding, and patience. Coverage classification (man vs. zone, and shells like Cover 1/2/3/4/6) is hand-charted or tracking-derived per play — it does not exist in standard play-by-play. Underlying data: @FantasyPtsData (Fantasy Points Data Suite — every snap hand-charted, coverage scheme among its charted fields; attribution UNVERIFIED per the truncation note above).
2. WHERE TO FIND IT — Post: https://x.com/sfdata9ers/status/2113749051917095414 . Primary source: Fantasy Points Data Suite — https://fantasypointsdata.com/ (paid suite; coverage, pressure, personnel splits and EPA, exportable). Free partial analogue: FTN charting via nflverse (blitz/play-action/screen/motion and interception-worthy flags — but NOT a man/zone coverage label; see §3).
3. HOW TO USE IT — This is the one chart in the batch GSE cannot fully reproduce from free data: nflverse play-by-play has no coverage label, and the free FTN charting subset in nflverse does not include man/zone classification. Options: (a) buy the Fantasy Points Data Suite and pull EPA/DB by coverage split directly (their suite advertises exactly these filters: coverage scheme, pressure, personnel, with EPA); (b) approximate coverage from NGS/tracking proxies — not legally/freely available at play level to the public; (c) approximate at TEAM level instead of play level: a defense's season man-rate is published by several charting outlets in summary form, so GSE can weight a QB's overall EPA/DB by the opponent's man/zone tendency without owning play-level labels — a tendency-weighted estimate, explicitly not the charted split. Inputs for (c): QB EPA/DB from `load_pbp()` + opponent man-rate from a charting summary.
4. WHERE TO USE IT — GSE prediction engine, **matchups layer** — this is a direct QB-vs-scheme interaction term: QB coverage-split delta × opponent coverage tendency. It extends the matchup layer beyond "QB EPA vs. defense EPA allowed" to "QB EPA vs. THIS KIND of defense," which is where the reasoning doctrine says plays should be formed. Second home: GSE Content OS — "coverage-proof" is a clean public-facing frame for a QB ranking piece (tease the frame, not the picks).
5. CREATIVE ANGLES — (a) Mirror it for receivers: WR EPA/target and YPRR vs. man vs. zone (Fantasy Points Data charts both) — a zone-beater WR facing a zone-heavy defense is the prop version of this same idea, and it pairs with the YPRR dossier in this batch (2116224110833360921). (b) Use the split delta as a stability flag: a QB whose production is one-coverage-only is fragile against a coordinator who can change the mix mid-game — a live-betting / second-half angle (ties to the standing 360° card's 1H-subset release). (c) Combine with pressure splits (Penix dossier, 2116303527024959750): coverage weakness × pressure weakness identifies the two levers a defense actually has against a given QB.
6. RISKS AND LIMITS — Man/zone is not binary in real defenses (match/pattern-match zone plays like man after the snap); vendors chart the boundary differently, so splits are not portable across sources. Per-QB per-coverage samples through mid-season are a few hundred dropbacks at most, split two ways — noisy. A QB's split also reflects his receivers' separation skills, not only the QB. All values in this post image-locked / UNVERIFIED; attribution to FantasyPtsData probable but truncated.
7. FIRST TEST AND VERDICT — Smallest real test: the team-tendency approximation in §3(c) — take one upcoming matchup where the defense's man-rate is extreme (top-3 or bottom-3), compute the QB's raw EPA/DB, and log whether the tendency-weighted read would have changed the matchup grade versus the unweighted read; score it after the game. Verdict: **TEST** — the interaction is mechanically sound and decision-relevant; test the free approximation before any suite purchase is even discussed.

### #68 — @sfdata9ers — 2116224110833360921
Post: https://x.com/sfdata9ers/status/2116224110833360921
TEXT RECOVERY: FULL for the post text — recovered from the per-account raw file (2026-10-30-midday-x100/sfdata9ers.json): "Yards per Route Run Leaders — Data: @FantasyPtsData, min. 70 routes". The leaderboard values themselves are in the chart image and were recorded in the raw file as IMAGE VALUES UNREADABLE — every name/value on the chart is UNVERIFIED / image-locked and none is quoted here.

1. WHAT IT IS — Yards per Route Run (YPRR) = a receiver's total receiving yards ÷ routes run, where a "route" is a play on which the player went out on a pass pattern (charted participation data — a player on the field for a pass play who blocks did not run a route). It is the standard per-opportunity receiving efficiency metric because it normalizes for playing time and team pass volume in one step: a 60-yard game on 15 routes (4.00 YPRR) is a different player from 60 yards on 35 routes (1.71). Reference bands from the metric's literature: ~2.00+ is elite for a WR over a real sample, ~1.4–1.99 is starter-grade, league-average WR sits near ~1.3–1.5 (bands vary by source/era — treat as orientation, not constants). This post's floor is min. 70 routes. Underlying data: @FantasyPtsData (Fantasy Points Data Suite charting — routes are a charted participation field).
2. WHERE TO FIND IT — Post: https://x.com/sfdata9ers/status/2116224110833360921 . Primary source: https://fantasypointsdata.com/ (paid). Metric definition reference (open): https://www.fantasypros.com/2023/06/yards-per-route-run-yprr-fantasy-football/ . Free route data does exist in one place: the FTN charting file in nflverse carries route/participation-adjacent fields (see the FTN dictionary, dossier 2095240387472273741) — coverage of "routes run" per player per play should be confirmed in that dictionary before relying on it.
3. HOW TO USE IT — Formula needs two inputs: receiving yards (free, exact, in nflverse player stats / play-by-play) and routes run (charted — the scarce input). GSE's options: (a) Fantasy Points Data Suite subscription — direct YPRR and targets-per-route-run with man/zone splits; (b) FTN charting via `nflreadr::load_ftn_charting()` — check its participation fields for a routes denominator; (c) crude proxy — yards per team dropback while on field, using snap counts (`load_snap_counts()`) × pass-play share: this is yards per pass-snap, NOT YPRR, and must be labeled as such if used. Stabilization warning from the metric's own literature: YPRR needs roughly 180+ routes to stabilize for WRs — this post's 70-route floor is well under that, so its leaders list is a form table, not a talent table.
4. WHERE TO USE IT — GSE prediction engine, **usage layer** (receiver opportunity/efficiency inputs to receiving props) with a secondary feed into the **matchups layer** when split by coverage (see 2113749051917095414). It extends the usage layer's target-share reasoning: target share says how often a QB looks at a receiver; YPRR says what a route is worth when he does. Direct product use: DFS packs (RedZone DFS) — high-YPRR / low-route-share players are the classic "role grows or one injury away" tournament leverage archetype.
5. CREATIVE ANGLES — (a) YPRR × route-share quadrant as a standing weekly Content OS chart: high YPRR + rising route share is the breakout signature, publishable as a category tease without giving a prop away. (b) Combine with the coverage-split dossier: YPRR vs. man and vs. zone separately (the data suite supports it) turns one leaderboard into a matchup tool. (c) For the research corpus: YPRR is one of the few fantasy metrics with a documented year-over-year correlation story (PFF's sophomore-WR work reported YPRR's next-year fantasy correlation well above other efficiency stats) — a candidate for GSE's own stability audit on nflverse-era data.
6. RISKS AND LIMITS — 70-route minimum is below the metric's stabilization range; early-season leaders regress hard. Routes are charted, and route counts differ slightly between charting vendors (what counts as a route on a broken play / scramble drill is a judgment call). YPRR confounds receiver skill with QB quality, target depth (deep-route receivers run lower-percentage, higher-yield routes), and role. This post's actual leaders: UNVERIFIED / image-locked.
7. FIRST TEST AND VERDICT — Smallest real test: compute the yards-per-pass-snap proxy (§3c) for all WRs with 70+ pass snaps from free nflverse data, and check rank correlation against any one published YPRR list Garrett already has in-corpus; if the proxy ranks correlate strongly, GSE has a free working substitute. Verdict: **LEARN-ONLY** (metric itself is ADOPT-grade knowledge for the usage layer; this post's list is not usable data).
