The chart's per-team values are image-locked (raw file: IMAGE VALUES UNREADABLE) — UNVERIFIED, none quoted here.

1. WHAT IT IS — A team-defense chart splitting success rate allowed into its two play-type components. Success rate allowed = share of opponent plays that succeed against this defense, where (per the standard definitions in dossier 2112260996941414825) a play succeeds on the 40/60/100 yardage rule or, in EPA implementations, has EPA > 0. Split by pass plays vs. run plays, it answers the funnel question: is this defense beatable through the air, on the ground, both, or neither? A defense can post a middling overall success rate allowed while being bottom-five against the pass and top-five against the run — the split is where the matchup value lives, because offenses choose their mix. Underlying data: @FantasyPtsData charting per the post credit; note the underlying success calculation itself is computable from ordinary play-by-play (see §3) — the vendor credit here is for the chart's play-type/success implementation, whose exact success definition is not stated in the recovered text (UNVERIFIED which of the two definitions in §1 was used).
2. WHERE TO FIND IT — Post: https://x.com/sfdata9ers/status/2116204984706707742 . Primary source: https://fantasypointsdata.com/ . Open reproduction path: nflverse play-by-play — https://github.com/nflverse/nflreadr .
3. HOW TO USE IT — Fully reproducible in-house, as the seen-index note itself said: `load_pbp()` gives every play with `epa`, play type, and posteam/defteam. Defensive pass success rate allowed = mean(success) over opponent dropbacks (attempts + sacks + scrambles, per nflverse conventions); defensive rush success rate allowed = mean(success) over opponent designed runs (exclude QB kneels/spikes; decide and document whether QB scrambles count as pass or run — nflfastR convention treats scrambles as pass plays). Using the EPA>0 success definition makes this a one-line group-by; using 40/60/100 requires computing gain vs. yards-to-go by down, also fully available in the same file. No paid input needed.
4. WHERE TO USE IT — GSE prediction engine, **matchups layer** — this is a core defensive-profile input: opponent pass-SR-allowed vs. GSE's pass offense grade, and rush-SR-allowed vs. run offense grade, instead of one blended defensive number. It makes the matchup layer cheaper and more honest at the same time: it replaces any need to buy a vendor chart for this stat, and it forces the reasoning trail to name HOW a defense is beatable, which is the reasoning-doctrine requirement (the published Why must be the matchup reasoning, not a market echo). Secondary: situations layer — a defense whose rush success allowed spikes in the red zone / short yardage is a goal-line tendency input.
5. CREATIVE ANGLES — (a) Difference chart: pass-SR-allowed minus rush-SR-allowed per defense = a "funnel index" — positive-funnel defenses push opponents to pass (inflates QB attempt props, deflates RB carry props) and vice versa; that single derived number writes its own weekly content segment. (b) Split further by down: early-down rush success allowed is the stay-on-schedule stat that predicts whether an underdog can shorten a game — a totals angle. (c) Cross with the stuff-rate dossier in this batch (2116258849829363769): stuff rate is the extreme tail of rush failure; rush success allowed is the whole distribution — using both separates "boom-or-bust run defense" from "consistently soft run defense."
6. RISKS AND LIMITS — Success rate is not opponent-adjusted by default; a defense that faced three elite rushing teams looks soft on the ground. Game script contaminates the split (teams protecting leads face pass-heavy mixes). The vendor's success definition is unstated here, so GSE's reproduction will not match the chart number-for-number — match the method, not the image. Chart values UNVERIFIED / image-locked.
7. FIRST TEST AND VERDICT — Smallest real test: one script, current season to date, both definitions, all 32 defenses, pass/rush split — then sanity-check the extremes against team EPA-allowed splits from the same file (they should agree in direction). Verdict: **TEST** — zero data cost, direct matchup-layer input; adopt into the layer if the extremes check out.

### #71 — @ghatz_28 — 2115464895361630232
Post: https://x.com/ghatz_28/status/2115464895361630232
TEXT RECOVERY: FULL — recovered verbatim from two per-account raw files (2026-10-30-midday-x100/ghatz.json and 2026-10-30-extra100/ghatz_28.json): "This is actually total snap %. So Smith is just playing more total snaps than Hendrickson and Green." The post is a correction/reply about a chart by another account (the seen-index identifies it as an ON3 pass-rusher chart); the original chart itself was not recovered in this batch and its values are UNVERIFIED.

1. WHAT IT IS — A chart-reading correction, and the mechanism is a denominator error — the single most common way sports charts mislead. The original chart (per the seen-index: an ON3 pass-rusher chart) apparently presented an axis or ranking that read as pass-rush usage/effectiveness for Smith vs. Trey Hendrickson and Mike Green (Baltimore's edge rotation in the surrounding coverage of that week). The correction: the axis is TOTAL snap percentage — the player's share of ALL defensive snaps (run downs + pass downs + everything) — not pass-rush snaps or pass-rush snap percentage. Therefore Smith's higher position on the chart is a playing-time artifact: he is on the field more in total, which mechanically inflates any counting or share stat built on total snaps, without saying anything about how often he rushes the passer or how well. The three denominators that must never be confused: total defensive snaps, pass-rush snaps (snaps on which the player actually rushed), and team pass plays faced. Pressure rate (pressures ÷ pass-rush snaps) is only meaningful on the second.
2. WHERE TO FIND IT — Post: https://x.com/ghatz_28/status/2115464895361630232 . Primary sources for doing it right: snap counts are free in nflverse — `nflreadr::load_snap_counts()` (PFR-sourced, game-level, 2012–present) via https://github.com/nflverse/nflreadr ; pass-rush-specific snap/pressure charting is a PFF/NGS-class product (NGS publishes pressure rate on pass-rush snaps for leaders — https://nextgenstats.nfl.com/ ).
3. HOW TO USE IT — As a rule, not a dataset: every rate that enters GSE carries its denominator in its name and definition (per-play, per-dropback, per-route, per-coverage-snap, per-pass-rush-snap). In code terms: nflverse snap counts give total snaps by player/game free; pass-rush snaps require charting (PFF) or NGS leaderboards, so any GSE pass-rush rate built only on free data must be labeled pressures-per-total-snap or pressures-per-team-pass-play — an approximation with the denominator stated, never silently "pressure rate." This dossier's practical output is a checklist item for the engine's data dictionary and for Content OS chart review.
4. WHERE TO USE IT — GSE prediction engine, **matchups layer** (pass-rush vs. pass-protection inputs — the exact place a total-snap % masquerading as rush usage would corrupt a grade), and GSE Content OS quality control (every chart GSE publishes or cites gets a denominator check before it is used as reasoning). It replaces nothing; it is a guardrail that makes the pass-rush inputs already in the stack trustworthy. Also the research corpus: this is a clean, citable example of the denominator-artifact failure mode for the corpus's chart-literacy file.
5. CREATIVE ANGLES — (a) Turn the correction into content: "the chart was wrong, here's the right denominator" is a credibility-building post format that fits GSE's reasoning-first brand — detect the artifact, show the corrected ranking. (b) Apply the same audit to the other dossiers in this batch: YPRR's 70-route floor (2116224110833360921) and the CB 150-coverage-snap floor (2112640012227645625) are both denominator choices — the audit question "what denominator, and who does it exclude?" is one habit across all three. (c) A fleet-wide lint: any agent-produced chart in the Sports repo could be scanned for axis labels lacking a denominator — a cheap automated QA step for the agent fleet infrastructure.
6. RISKS AND LIMITS — The correction is itself a creator claim about someone else's chart; the original ON3 chart was not recovered in this batch, so what its axis actually said is UNVERIFIED — the dossier records the correction as ghatz_28's stated reading, and the general denominator lesson stands regardless of who was right in the specific dispute. Names (Smith / Hendrickson / Green) come from the post text and seen-index; which "Smith" is meant is not specified in the recovered text and is not guessed here.
7. FIRST TEST AND VERDICT — Smallest real test: none needed as a data test — the actionable test is procedural: pick one pass-rush chart already in GSE's corpus, state its denominator in one sentence; if the sentence cannot be written from the chart's labels, the chart is downgraded to context-only. Verdict: **LEARN-ONLY** — a method lesson, cataloged permanently; nothing gets built from the disputed chart itself.

### #72 — @PattonAnalytics — 2116258849829363769
Post: https://x.com/PattonAnalytics/status/2116258849829363769
TEXT RECOVERY: FULL — recovered verbatim from per-account raw files (2026-10-30-extra100/PattonAnalytics.json; also 2026-10-30-pm/PattonAnalytics.txt): "Defensive Stuff Rate Leaders, via @StatRankings: 1. Bengals (23.1%) 2. Steelers (21.7%) 3. Browns (21.1%) 4. Bills (20.8%) 5. Seahawks (20.6%) 6. Packers (20%) 7. Ravens (19.8%) 8. Buccaneers (19.4%) 9. Vikings (19.3%) 10. Falcons (18.7%)". Values are in the post text; they are @StatRankings' figures as relayed by the creator — see §6 on the definition check.

1. WHAT IT IS — Defensive stuff rate = opponent rushing plays stopped at or behind the line of scrimmage (gain ≤ 0 yards) ÷ opponent rushing plays. It is the run-defense disruption rate: not how many yards a defense allows per carry on average, but how often it wins the play outright at the snap — penetration, TFLs, and run blitzes made visible. The classic Football Outsiders "stuffed" definition counts carries stopped at or before the line; implementations differ on the denominator (all opponent rushes vs. RB carries only vs. designed runs excluding QB scrambles/kneels) — @StatRankings' exact denominator for this table is not stated in the post and is UNVERIFIED; GSE must fix its own definition (§3) rather than assume theirs. Season-to-date at the post (Oct 30, 2026), the spread from #1 Cincinnati (23.1%) to #10 Atlanta (18.7%) is 4.4 points — meaningful but not enormous; league-average stuff rate historically sits near ~19–20% on the FO-style definition (orientation only).
2. WHERE TO FIND IT — Post: https://x.com/PattonAnalytics/status/2116258849829363769 . Primary source: StatRankings — https://statrankings.com/nfl/advanced/teams/trench-play/havoc-rate (the site's advanced team-stats hub; its published methodology pages confirm figures are recalculated from play-by-play after each game — the specific stuff-rate page was not individually retrieved in this run, so the citation is to the StatRankings advanced-stats section, and the metric is independently reproducible per §3).
3. HOW TO USE IT — Fully computable free from nflverse play-by-play: filter opponent designed runs (play_type = run, exclude kneels; document the scramble/QB-run handling), stuff = yards_gained ≤ 0, rate = stuffs ÷ runs, group by defteam. `load_pbp()` via https://github.com/nflverse/nflreadr . One script produces the full 32-team table for any window (season, last-3-weeks — the window @sfdata9ers used for its own run-stuff chart elsewhere in the corpus). No vendor subscription needed; StatRankings becomes a cross-check, not a dependency.
4. WHERE TO USE IT — GSE prediction engine, **matchups layer** — the run-game leg: defense stuff rate vs. offense stuffed-rate-allowed is the early-down matchup that decides whether a run game stays on schedule (see the rush-success split dossier, 2116204984706707742 — stuff rate is that distribution's left tail). Concrete uses: RB rushing props (a top-3 stuff defense is a carries-efficiency downgrade), team totals for run-heavy underdogs (stuff rate is how a favorite's clock-control game plan dies), and goal-line/short-yardage reasoning in the situations layer. It extends the matchup layer with a play-level disruption stat that yards-per-carry allowed hides (a defense can allow 4.6 YPC built on either steady 4s or a mix of stuffs and explosives — those are different matchups).
5. CREATIVE ANGLES — (a) Stuff rate × explosive-run rate allowed as a two-axis run-defense profile: high-stuff/high-explosive = gambling front (favors RB longest-rush overs and hurts RB rushing-yards median), low-stuff/low-explosive = bend-don't-break (favors carry-volume props). (b) Offensive mirror: team stuffed-rate-allowed as an offensive-line health metric — it moves faster than total rushing EPA when a line loses a starter, so it is an early-warning input to the health layer. (c) Content OS: a weekly "trench report" (stuff rate for/against per matchup) is a differentiated, non-pick public content lane that no tout account in the feed is running.
6. RISKS AND LIMITS — Denominator ambiguity (§1) means GSE's table will not exactly match StatRankings' — publish GSE's definition with GSE's table. Stuff rate is scheme- and script-sensitive: defenses facing run-heavy, trailing teams see more predictable runs. It says nothing about second-level tackling (a stuff-or-15-yards defense can post a fine stuff rate). Sample through late October is ~250–300 opponent rushes per team — a 2-point gap is within noise; treat the top-10 list as tiers, not ranks. Values as relayed are creator-relayed vendor figures, UNVERIFIED against StatRankings directly in this run.
7. FIRST TEST AND VERDICT — Smallest real test: compute the table from nflverse for the same season-to-date window under the designed-runs definition and compare the top 10 membership (not exact percentages) to the post's list; ≥7 of 10 matching means the in-house version is a working substitute. Verdict: **TEST** — free to compute, directly feeds the matchup layer's run leg.

### #74 — @nfl_simulator — 2095240387472273741
Post: https://x.com/nfl_simulator/status/2095240387472273741
TEXT RECOVERY: FULL — recovered verbatim from the per-account raw file (2026-10-30-deepmath/w_nfl_simulator.json): "Denver's 2024 defense caught 13 of 17 interceptable throws (77%). The model shrunk their estimated interceptable catch rate to ~55%. In 2025 Denver caught 8 of 21 (38%), so the shrinkage provides a better estimate." A companion post by the same account in the same raw file (2095496231975891271, context, not a separate dossier) states the underlying charting source: "The dropped-pass and interceptable-throw charting used for model training and predictions comes from @FTNFantasy (via #nflverse)".

1. WHAT IT IS — A textbook empirical-Bayes / hierarchical shrinkage demonstration on a rare-event rate. Definitions: an "interceptable throw" is a pass charted as interception-worthy (FTN charting field `is_interception_worthy` — a pass the charting team judged could/should have been intercepted, whether or not it was). A defense's "interceptable catch rate" = actual interceptions ÷ interceptable throws — i.e., how often the defense converted its chances. Denver 2024: 13/17 = 77% raw. The model shrinks that toward the league-wide prior distribution of catch rates (Beta-binomial / hierarchical posterior mean = (α + catches) ÷ (α + β + chances), where α, β are prior pseudo-counts estimated from all teams' data): the shrunk estimate is ~55%. Why: with only 17 chances, the raw 77% is mostly sampling noise — the posterior blends the team's 17 observations with the league prior, weighting the prior heavily at small n. Validation built into the post: in 2025 Denver caught 8/21 = 38% — the shrunk 55% was much closer to the next-season realized rate than the raw 77% was (and 38% itself would then be shrunk upward toward the prior; shrinkage works in both directions). The general law: raw rates on small denominators are biased AWAY from the mean in any leaderboard, and the extremes of any rate leaderboard are disproportionately small-sample teams/players.
2. WHERE TO FIND IT — Post: https://x.com/nfl_simulator/status/2095240387472273741 . Primary sources: FTN charting data dictionary in nflverse (defines `is_interception_worthy`, `is_drop`, and the other charted fields, data from 2022 onward) — https://nflreadr.nflverse.com/articles/dictionary_ftn_charting.html ; loader: https://github.com/nflverse/nflreadr (`load_ftn_charting()`). Method reference for empirical Bayes: https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Empirical_Bayes_method .
3. HOW TO USE IT — Fully implementable with free data GSE can legally get: `load_ftn_charting(seasons)` joined to play-by-play on game/play IDs gives every interception-worthy pass since 2022; flag whether the play ended in an interception (from PBP). Fit the league prior by method of moments or MLE on all teams' (catches, chances), then posterior-mean each team/player. The same one function — shrink(rate, n, prior) — generalizes to every unstable rate in the engine: fumble-recovery rate, red-zone TD rate, fourth-down conversion rate, kicker FG% by distance band, onside/recovery luck, dropped-pass rate (FTN `is_drop` is in the same file). Inputs: FTN charting + PBP only. This is a few dozen lines, not a project.
4. WHERE TO USE IT — GSE prediction engine, **everything-else layer** as a cross-cutting estimator (it is not a matchup signal; it is the method by which ALL small-sample rate inputs to usage / health / matchups / situations get stabilized before they are used), with its first application in the **matchups layer**: defensive turnover expectation. It replaces the engine's implicit use of raw turnover rates — raw INT totals are one of the least stable defensive stats, and a defense priced off a 77%-conversion season is a regression fade the engine should find mechanically. Also the research corpus: this post is the cleanest worked shrinkage example with a built-in out-of-sample check (2024 estimate → 2025 outcome) in the entire batch.
5. CREATIVE ANGLES — (a) Turnover-luck index for content: shrunk-vs-raw gap per defense = "luckiest defenses" list — a weekly Content OS segment and a totals/sides reasoning input (lucky defenses regress → their opponents' team totals are systematically underpriced early the next season). (b) Apply shrinkage to GSE's OWN track record presentation: shrunk win-rate estimates by market type would keep the packs' marketing honest on small samples — credibility as a feature. (c) Extend the same hierarchical machinery to the fleet's other rate problems: agent success rates on task types, content hit rates by format — one estimator, many homes, including the model-routing stack's per-model success rates on small prompt samples.
6. RISKS AND LIMITS — Shrinkage is only as good as the prior: pooling all defenses assumes exchangeability (a defense with elite ball-hawk DBs genuinely converts more; the hierarchical model handles this only if the prior variance is estimated honestly, not set to force everyone to the mean). FTN's "interception-worthy" is a charting judgment — charting drift between seasons would contaminate the pool. The post's validation is a single team-season (n=1 anecdote): 55% being closer than 77% for Denver 2025 is consistent with shrinkage working, not proof of the specific prior chosen. The ~55% figure is the creator's model output — the prior's parameters are not stated, so the exact number is UNVERIFIED even though the method and the raw counts (13/17, 8/21) are checkable in the FTN file.
7. FIRST TEST AND VERDICT — Smallest real test: pull FTN charting 2022–2025, compute every defense's raw interceptable catch rate in year Y and its shrunk estimate, then score both against year Y+1 realized rates (MAE across team-seasons). If shrunk beats raw out-of-sample — the published claim, tested on the full panel instead of one team — the estimator goes into the engine. Verdict: **TEST** — highest method-value-per-line-of-code in this batch; the test is cheap, free-data, and decisive.

1. WHAT IT IS — A pressure-sensitivity profile for Atlanta Falcons QB Michael Penix Jr.: his EPA per dropback split by whether the defense pressured him on the play. Pressure = a defender's pressure event on the dropback (hurry/hit/sack per the charting vendor — here SumerSports' tracking/charting). The split reported across multiple independent outlets covering the same 2026 season (ESPN research as quoted by Sporting News / USA Today / SB Nation Falcons sites): Penix's EPA/dropback when NOT pressured was approximately +0.58 to +0.62 (2nd–5th best in the NFL depending on the week cited), and when pressured approximately −0.70 to −0.82 (worst / 4th-worst among qualifiers) — a clean-vs-pressured gap of roughly 1.3–1.4 EPA per dropback, among the largest recorded splits; one report added he completed 1 of 16 passes (6%) under pressure in the sampled window, the only starter under 30%. Exact figures vary by week and source — treat the PATTERN (elite clean, worst pressured, extreme gap) as corroborated and every specific decimal as source-and-window-specific, UNVERIFIED for this post's chart. The mechanism: a stationary pocket passer with high-end arm talent whose processing and accuracy collapse when the pocket moves — pressure sensitivity is a QB trait estimate, and the actionable quantity is the GAP, not either side alone.
2. WHERE TO FIND IT — Post: https://x.com/SumerSports/status/2116303527024959750 . Primary source: SumerSports — https://www.sumersports.com (their tracking/charting produces pressure splits). Corroborating published coverage located in this run: https://ftw.usatoday.com/story/sports/nfl/2026/10/12/michael-penix-jr-one-best-nfl-qbs-falcons-2026-1-big-caveat/92225636007/ and https://www.sportingnews.com/us/nfl/chicago-bears/news/why-bears-might-big-trouble-falcons-michael-penix-jr-week-6/3e21e524fb8cd4678285aba9 (both quoting ESPN research figures).
3. HOW TO USE IT — The split itself needs pressure charting, which free nflverse PBP does not carry directly — BUT the free FTN charting file in nflverse carries adjacent fields (n_pass_rushers, n_blitzers, is_qb_fault_sack, QB out-of-pocket) and PFR advanced passing carries per-QB pressure stats in aggregated form; a true play-level pressured/not EPA split is a PFF / NGS / Sumer-class charted product. GSE's practical approximations: (a) QB EPA/DB vs. blitz vs. no-blitz from FTN charting (blitz is charted free; pressure correlates with but is not identical to blitz); (b) QB EPA/DB split by opponent defensive pressure rate (a defense-level proxy — the QB faces a high-pressure ENVIRONMENT); (c) sack rate + time-to-throw proxies from NGS aggregates via `load_nextgen_stats()`. The decision-relevant composite GSE can build today: QB pressure-sensitivity proxy × opponent pressure rate = expected EPA drag — computable from (a)/(b) without a purchase.
4. WHERE TO USE IT — GSE prediction engine, **matchups layer** — this is a named, quantified interaction: QB pressure sensitivity × defensive pressure generation. It directly extends two inputs GSE already reasons about separately (QB quality, pass rush) into the product term that decides games like the ATL@TB preview in this same batch (2115726595293897128, which predicted Tampa's pass rush would force Penix into long third downs — this dossier is the trait data underneath that script). Player-prop expression: Penix passing yards/attempts and ATL team total downgrades scale with the opponent's pressure rate, not with the opponent's raw defensive rank. Also the **situations layer**: pressure sensitivity rises in obvious-passing situations, so third-and-long frequency against this QB is a live, countable in-game signal for the 1H-subset release.
5. CREATIVE ANGLES — (a) League-wide pressure-sensitivity leaderboard as a standing engine table: every starting QB gets a clean/pressured gap; the gap × next opponent's pressure rate is a one-number weekly matchup grade that writes its own reasoning sentence ("QB loses ~1.3 EPA/DB under pressure; opponent pressures on X% — the pass rush IS the matchup"). (b) Offensive-line health interaction: a pressure-sensitive QB behind a downgraded OL (health layer) is a multiplicative, not additive, fade — the engine should flag the co-occurrence explicitly. (c) Content OS: "the most pressure-sensitive QB in football" is a repeatable, checkable superlative format — each week the leaderboard's extreme gets a tease post (category only, no pick). (d) Combine with the coverage-split dossier (2113749051917095414): coverage weakness tells you WHICH lever (man/zone mix) a defense has; pressure sensitivity tells you the OTHER lever (heat) — together they are a complete "how to defend this QB" card.

RISKS AND LIMITS — Pressured-dropback samples are small (a QB may have only 40–80 pressured dropbacks at mid-season) and the pressured EPA figure is mechanically dragged by sacks, which are partly OL and scheme, not QB trait — the split confounds QB, line, and play-calling (a team that calls slow-developing routes manufactures its own pressure). The corroborating figures come from different weeks/sources and disagree at the decimal level; the post's own chart values are UNVERIFIED / image-locked. Pressure charting definitions differ by vendor (PFF vs. NGS vs. Sumer will not match). Trait stability year-over-year is real but imperfect — young QBs do improve under pressure, so the estimate must decay/refresh rather than harden into a label.
7. FIRST TEST AND VERDICT — Smallest real test: build approximation (a) from FTN charting + PBP for all QBs with 150+ dropbacks this season (EPA/DB vs. blitz / no-blitz as the free pressure proxy), check that Penix's proxy gap ranks at or near the league extreme as the charted reports say his true gap does; if the free proxy reproduces the ranking's extremes, GSE owns a working version of this input at zero cost. Verdict: **TEST** — the trait is corroborated, the matchup interaction is exactly GSE's reasoning product, and the free proxy test is decisive and cheap.

The mechanism behind every such claim, stated plainly: a hit rate without (a) the number of plays, (b) the time window, (c) the closing lines the plays were graded against, and (d) the full unedited record including losers is not a statistic — 100% over 4 plays, 100% over a cherry-picked window, and 100% after deleting losers are all "100%." Cheat-sheet hit rates are also usually computed on the prop's recent game log ("has hit in 10 of his last 10") — a backward-looking streak stat about the PLAYER's game log, repackaged as if it were the SELLER's prediction record. Those are different things: a player clearing a line set for THIS week's matchup in his last 10 games against other defenses is a descriptive streak, and the current line already reflects it. No model, dataset, or method is named in the recovered text.

The remaining ranks are UNVERIFIED / not recovered; nothing past "R" is asserted here.

1. WHAT IT IS — A Kansas City defensive-efficiency summary built on the two standard play-level efficiency metrics. EPA/play allowed: for every play run against KC's defense, EPA = EP(after) − EP(before) from the offense's perspective using the nflfastR-class expected-points model (EP from down, distance, yard line, time/score); "allowed" flips the sign convention so lower is better, and KC ranking 3rd means only two defenses concede fewer expected points per play. Success rate allowed: a play is a "success" for the offense if it gains ≥40% of yards-to-go on 1st down, ≥60% on 2nd, or 100% on 3rd/4th (the standard nflfastR `success` definition); KC ranking 2nd in success rate allowed means offenses succeed at the second-lowest rate against them. The pairing matters: EPA/play is explosive-play-sensitive, success rate is down-to-down consistency — top-3 in both says the defense is both stingy and non-leaky, not merely bend-don't-break. Underlying data: play-by-play (the metrics are computable from public PBP; PFF's post is a republication of standard quantities, whatever PFF's internal EPA source).
2. WHERE TO FIND IT — Post: https://x.com/PFF/status/2112664153500606836 . Primary source for the metric construction: the nflfastR EP/WP/CP model documentation https://opensourcefootball.com/posts/2020-09-28-nflfastr-ep-wp-and-cp-models/ ; computable data: nflverse play-by-play via nflreadr/nflreadpy (reference index https://nflreadr.nflverse.com/reference/index.html ).
3. HOW TO USE IT — GSE computes both today from nflverse PBP: filter defensive plays for KC, mean `epa`, mean `success`, rank across 32 defenses, Weeks 1–8 (the post's apparent window, "this season" as of Oct 30). State the garbage-time filter (WP in [0.05, 0.95] is the common choice) because defensive ranks move with it, and publish raw and opponent-adjusted versions side by side — the post's version is unadjusted. No paid PFF subscription is needed for these two metrics.
4. WHERE TO USE IT — GSE prediction engine, **matchups layer** (defensive rating input) — this is the engine's bread-and-butter team-strength pair, and the post is a reminder to carry EPA/play allowed and success rate allowed as *separate* features rather than one blended defensive grade: their divergence (good EPA rank, bad success rank = explosive-dependent defense) is itself a matchup signal vs explosive vs methodical offenses. It extends, not replaces, GSE's existing EPA stack.
5. CREATIVE ANGLES — (a) The EPA-rank vs success-rank gap as a named archetype table for all 32 defenses ("fortress" = top-10 both, "mirage" = EPA propped by turnovers/red-zone luck while success rate bleeds) — a weekly Content OS chart that gives away no pick. (b) Pair defensive success rate allowed with the red-zone dossier in this batch (2116230653792391634): a defense can lose the success battle between the 20s and win points-per-trip inside them — the two together explain total-points outcomes better than either alone, a totals-reasoning input formed matchup-first. (c) Third-down-specific success rate allowed is the natural bridge to the situations layer (3rd-down defense × opponent 3rd-down offense).

The approximation differs from the charted original in one respect: official air yards are measured to the catch/incompletion point as scored, while Fantasy Points charting grades target location independently — close enough for distribution shape, not identical play-by-play. All 32 team values are in the chart: UNVERIFIED / image-locked. (The companion offensive chart's partial ordering was visible in the same pull — SF 1st, NO 2nd, BUF 3rd … GB 32nd on offense — but that is a different post and is not asserted as this post's defensive values.)

1. WHAT IT IS — A defensive red-zone efficiency ranking with an explicit, well-chosen construction: points per red zone trip allowed = total points conceded on drives that reached the defense's red zone ÷ number of such trips, through Week 7. A "trip" is a drive with at least one play inside (or a first down inside) the opponent's 20-yard line; points per trip blends TD rate (7-ish points), field-goal rate (3), and empty-trip rate (0 from turnovers on downs/missed kicks) into one number, league average sitting near ~4.5–5.0. It is a better red-zone metric than raw "RZ TD% allowed" because it prices the FG-vs-TD tradeoff and empty trips together. Underlying data: play-by-play drive reconstruction — no charting vendor required.
2. WHERE TO FIND IT — Post: https://x.com/sfdata9ers/status/2116230653792391634 . Primary source for the same quantity: nflverse play-by-play (drive IDs, yard lines, scores on every play) via nflreadr/nflreadpy, reference index https://nflreadr.nflverse.com/reference/index.html ; drive-level cross-checks at Pro Football Reference (cited as the snap/drive data origin in nflverse's own docs).
3. HOW TO USE IT — Fully computable by GSE from free nflverse PBP: group plays by `game_id` + `drive`, flag a defensive trip when the offense's `yardline_100 <= 20` on any play of the drive, sum the drive's offensive points (TD 6 + XP/2pt as scored + FG 3 — state the convention; the common one counts 7 per TD-with-kick via drive result), divide by trips, rank defenses. Publish the trip counts beside the rates: through 7 weeks a defense has faced roughly 20–30 RZ trips, so the rate's denominator must travel with it. Inputs: PBP only — data GSE already pulls.
4. WHERE TO USE IT — GSE prediction engine, **matchups layer** as the red-zone conversion feature inside team ratings, with its heaviest use in totals and team-total reasoning (points per trip is literally a scoring-rate input) and a **situations layer** feed (RZ defense × opponent RZ offense is a named situational matchup). It replaces the cruder "RZ TD% allowed" and raw points-allowed-per-game in the reasoning trail with a per-trip scoring expectation, and it pairs with the offensive mirror (points per RZ trip scored) for a symmetric two-sided feature.
5. CREATIVE ANGLES — (a) Split it: points per trip allowed *by trip origin* (trips starting from a short field after a turnover vs full-field drives) separates defensive quality from being hung out by the offense — the short-field split is close to a pure defense measure. (b) RZ trips *faced per game* is a field-position/pace stat in disguise: defenses facing few trips but allowing high points/trip are explosive-play defenses, a completely different totals profile than bend-but-don't-break teams with the same PPG. (c) Content OS: "best/worst RZ defenses by points per trip" with trip counts shown is a credibility-forward chart — it teaches the audience why GSE shows denominators, differentiating from rank-only accounts.
6. RISKS AND LIMITS — Small denominators: ~25 trips per defense through Week 7 means one goal-line stand swings the rate by ~0.25 pts/trip; treat ranks as tiers, not orderings. Points-per-trip conventions differ (XP counting, kneel-down drives that reach the 20, overtime) — cross-source comparisons must use one stated convention. Short-field trips inflate "allowed" for good defenses with turnover-prone offenses. Chart values UNVERIFIED / image-locked in this pass; the construction is verified from the post text.
7. FIRST TEST AND VERDICT — Smallest test: rebuild the through-Week-7 2026 table from nflverse PBP under a stated convention and check the Spearman rank correlation against the chart if a transcription becomes available; at minimum verify GSE's own top-5/bottom-5 tiers are stable across the two XP conventions. Verdict: **LEARN-ONLY** (construction adopted as the standard RZ form; this snapshot's values stay unverified).

1. WHAT IT IS — A man-coverage liability ranking: for each defensive back (charted coverage defender), take all plays where he was the targeted defender, compute the NFL passer rating on those attempts, and list the 30 worst (highest rating allowed), with a minimum of 21 targets to qualify. Passer rating is the standard formula: components from completion %, yards/attempt, TD %, INT % on the attempts against him, each clamped, combined as ((a+b+c+d)/6)×100, range 0–158.3. So "cooked" = QBs produce near-elite efficiency when throwing at this defender. The critical input — *which* defender was targeted/in coverage — is not in official play-by-play; it comes from Fantasy Points Data's hand charting (they chart coverage/target responsibility on every play).
2. WHERE TO FIND IT — Post: https://x.com/sfdata9ers/status/2116189009596879145 . Primary source: Fantasy Points Data Suite (charted coverage data; vendor states every play of every game is hand-charted across eight charting processes) https://fantasypointsdata.com/ .
3. HOW TO USE IT — GSE cannot compute the true version from free data: nflverse PBP names the receiver and passer, not the coverage defender; FTN charting via nflverse does not carry a targeted-defender ID either; PFR advanced defense gives player-level targets-against for defenders (a partial approximation — PFR's "targets" for a DB are charted by PFR) — nflverse distributes PFR advstats (`load_pfr_advstats` with `stat_type = "def"`), which is the closest free approximation and must be labeled a proxy with a different charting standard. The exact version requires a Fantasy Points Data (or PFF) subscription. Minimum-sample handling is part of the method: the post's 21-target floor is the creator's choice; GSE should report rating-allowed with target counts and shrink small samples toward the league mean rather than rank raw.
4. WHERE TO USE IT — GSE prediction engine, **matchups layer** — this is the individual-coverage-matchup input: WR1 vs a bottom-30 coverage DB is a concrete, namable matchup fact for the reasoning trail and for receiver-prop and DFS reasoning (usage × matchup). It extends GSE's team-level pass-defense ratings with the player-level layer the Reasoning Doctrine wants ("player vs player" named explicitly). It replaces generic "weak secondary" language with a quantified, sourced liability — when the data is licensed; the PFR proxy version replaces nothing and must be flagged as proxy-grade in any published reasoning.
5. CREATIVE ANGLES — (a) The funnel corollary: pair "most cooked" with target *rate* against (targets per coverage snap): a cooked DB whom QBs avoid (because of scheme help) is a different betting fact than one QBs hunt — cooked × hunted is the true exploit list. (b) Slot vs outside splits: slot defenders' ratings-allowed are structurally inflated by shorter throws/higher completion % — splitting the list by alignment turns a meme chart into a usable matchup table. (c) Content OS: a weekly "cooked list" is high-engagement, and GSE's version — with target counts and shrinkage shown — is the reasoning-brand version of it; tease the list shape, keep names/values for the paid pack where licensing allows.
6. RISKS AND LIMITS — Passer rating allowed conflates QB quality faced, receiver quality, pass rush (a beaten DB on a 4-second play), and scheme help; it is not an isolated coverage grade. 21 targets is a small sample: a single 80-yard TD on 21 targets moves the rating enormously. Charted "targeted defender" calls are judgment calls that differ between vendors (Fantasy Points vs PFF lists will not match). The entire list here is image-locked and UNVERIFIED. Repost (RT of own post) means the data window is older than the repost date — the underlying window is not stated in the recovered text.
7. FIRST TEST AND VERDICT — Smallest test: pull PFR advanced-defense targets-against via nflverse for the same approximate window, compute rating-allowed per DB with a 21-target floor, and see whether the proxy's worst-10 overlaps the archetype the chart implies (young/forced-start DBs) — the proxy is useful only if its extreme tail is stable week to week (split-half correlation). WHAT IT IS — A provenance note identifying FTN's charting operation as a source for two target-quality event types that official play-by-play does not record: (i) receiver drops — targets that hit the receiver and should have been caught, charged to the receiver rather than the QB; and (ii) plays where the targeted receiver was open — a charted separation/openness judgment on the target. Verified against the primary source: nflverse's FTN charting dictionary (the free FTN subset, 2022–present, CC-BY-SA 4.0) contains exactly this family of fields — `is_drop` ("receiver drop"), `is_catchable_ball` (throws generally on target and not defended away), `is_contested_ball`, `is_created_reception` (reception only due to an exceptional receiver play), `is_interception_worthy`, `read_thrown` (which progression read the throw went to). One gap: the dictionary excerpt does not show a literal field named "open" — FTN's openness information in the free subset is carried through the catchable/contested/created-reception family rather than a standalone open flag, so the post's "open" wording is verified in spirit (charted target-quality judgments exist) but the exact field mapping is UNVERIFIED.
2. WHERE TO FIND IT — Post: https://x.com/PattonAnalytics/status/2115882829699797301 . Primary source: FTN charting data dictionary as distributed by nflverse https://nflreadr.nflverse.com/articles/dictionary_ftn_charting.html ; access via `nflreadr::load_ftn_charting()` (R) / nflreadpy equivalent, seasons 2022+, free subset under CC-BY-SA 4.0 with FTN attribution.
3. HOW TO USE IT — Concrete and free: load FTN charting for 2026, join to nflverse PBP on game/play IDs, aggregate per receiver and per QB: drop rate = drops ÷ catchable targets; QB "helped/hurt" splits = catchable-ball rate on his targets vs his receivers' drop rate (separates QB accuracy from receiver hands — the exact attribution problem this charting exists to solve); contested/catchable shares per receiver as target-quality context for completion and YAC expectations. Latency: the free FTN file lands within ~48 hours of games per nflverse's data-source documentation — fine for a Monday-first weekly cycle. Inputs: PBP + FTN charting, both already in GSE's legal free stack.
4. WHERE TO USE IT — GSE prediction engine, **usage layer** (target quality adjusts how much a receiver's volume is worth) and **matchups layer** (QB accuracy measured on catchable balls vs a defense's contested-ball rate is a cleaner QB-vs-secondary matchup than raw completion %). It replaces raw drop counts scraped from box-score narratives and un-sourced "drops" claims in reasoning with a defined, reproducible field, and it extends GSE's existing FTN use (the corpus already uses FTN catchability/drops in weekly usage work) from ad hoc to a named standard: drops and target quality in GSE reasoning cite FTN charting via nflverse, with attribution.
5. CREATIVE ANGLES — (a) Drop-driven regression candidates: a receiver whose fantasy/props output is suppressed by a high drop rate on catchable balls has a *positive* expected regression (drops are among the least stable receiver stats) — a buy-low reasoning line for DFS packs that is fully computable today. (b) The inverse for QBs: completion % propped up by created receptions is borrowed production — flag QBs whose receivers lead in created-reception share before pricing their props. (c) Fleet/research corpus: this is the citation that settles "who charts drops" arguments in future sweeps — file it as the provenance answer so the next agent doesn't re-litigate it.
6. RISKS AND LIMITS — Charted judgments vary by charting team: FTN drops ≠ PFF drops ≠ Fantasy Points drops; numbers are not interchangeable across vendors, and GSE must name its vendor in print. The free FTN subset is a subset — some FTN fields are paid-only, and 2022+ coverage only. "Open" as a standalone grade is not confirmed in the free dictionary (see §1). CC-BY-SA 4.0 requires attribution and share-alike handling of derived data products — fine for internal reasoning and attributed publication, a real constraint for packaging raw data.
7. FIRST TEST AND VERDICT — Smallest test: load FTN charting 2026 Weeks 1–8, compute league drop rate on catchable targets and the top/bottom five receivers by drop rate (min 30 catchable targets), and sanity-check the leaders against known drop-prone names from independent reporting. Verdict: **TEST** (free, in-stack, immediately wireable into the usage layer — the test is a one-query wiring check).

### #85 — @KoalatyStats — 2115589616359072127
Post: https://x.com/KoalatyStats/status/2115589616359072127
TEXT RECOVERY: PARTIAL — full post text recovered from raw file (2026-10-30-midday-x100/KoalatyStats.json, Wed 28 Oct 2026 23:40:44 GMT): "2026 Expected Fantasy Points - Wide Receivers", with chart image present. Every player value in the chart is UNVERIFIED / image-locked.

1. WHAT IT IS — A season-to-date Expected Fantasy Points (xFP) leaderboard for wide receivers. xFP construction: for every target (and designed touch) a receiver gets, an expected-points model estimates the fantasy points an *average* player would score from that exact opportunity — using the opportunity's context: field position (distance to the end zone), target depth/air yards, down and distance, and play type. Operationally xFP = Σ over opportunities of [P(catch | context) × E(yards | catch, context) × pts/yard + P(TD | context) × 6 (+ 1 per expected reception in PPR)]. The credited open implementation in this creator's ecosystem is ffverse's ffopportunity (see the xTD dossier below): xgboost models trained on nflverse PBP (2006–2020) that output per-play expected receptions, yards, TDs, and fantasy points (`_exp` columns). The gap actual FP − xFP is the efficiency/luck residual: receivers far under xFP are regression-buy candidates; far over are sell-high. Koalaty's specific model settings are his own (he publishes via Substack, koalatystats.substack.com, and states his data sources as PFF/NGS/FantasyPts), so his chart's exact xFP flavor is creator-stated, UNVERIFIED in detail.
2. WHERE TO FIND IT — Post: https://x.com/KoalatyStats/status/2115589616359072127 . Primary sources: the open xFP implementation, ffverse/ffopportunity https://github.com/ffverse/ffopportunity/ (GPL-3.0 code; models/data CC BY-SA 4.0 per the project's data dictionary) with docs https://ffopportunity.ffverse.com/reference/ep_build.html ; creator's own outlet https://koalatystats.substack.com/p/week-7-advanced-stats-predictions .
3. HOW TO USE IT — GSE can build the identical table free: in R, `ffopportunity::ep_load(season = 2026, type = "weekly")` (precomputed) or `ep_build()` to apply the xgboost models to fresh nflverse PBP; aggregate `total_fantasy_points_exp` by receiver, filter position WR via rosters. A Python path exists through nflreadpy PBP + a locally fit gradient-boosting model on the same context features if the R dependency is unwanted. Inputs: PBP (air yards, yardline, down/distance all present) — no paid data required. Known defect to carry: ffopportunity issue #24 documents an `implied_total` reversal for home teams in preprocessing (acknowledged by maintainers, fix pending) — any feature touching team implied totals from this package must be patched or excluded.
4. WHERE TO USE IT — GSE **DFS packs / fantasy product line** first (xFP leaderboards and FP−xFP gap tables are core DFS-pack content and player-pool reasoning), and the prediction engine's **usage layer** second (xFP is the single-number summary of a receiver's opportunity quality — it feeds target-value reasoning for props). It replaces raw target counts as the usage layer's headline opportunity measure: 8 targets at the goal line ≠ 8 targets at midfield, and xFP is what prices that difference.
5. CREATIVE ANGLES — (a) Defense-side xFP: aggregate xFP *allowed* by defense and by coverage funnel (the corpus already reproduces Koalaty-style defensive funnels from PBP — the repro in the sweep archive computes WR/TE/RB target shares and PROE allowed for Weeks 1–7 2026) — xFP allowed per target by position group is a sharper funnel metric than target share alone. (b) xFP per route (needs route counts — FTN participation via nflverse gives routes for charted seasons) separates "great role" from "great per-route opportunity," the distinction DFS pricing misses most. (c) Content OS: a weekly "most unlucky WRs" (FP−xFP) post is evergreen engagement that advertises the DFS pack's method without giving away a lineup.
6. RISKS AND LIMITS — ffopportunity's models were trained on 2006–2020 football; league drift (passing rates, target depth) ages them, and the package's own data dictionary flags definitional choices (route participation is NOT in ffopportunity — `receptions_exp` is not a route proxy). Issue #24's implied-total bug is a live correctness risk in adjacent columns. Koalaty's chart values are image-locked/UNVERIFIED and his exact model variant is undisclosed. xFP measures opportunity, not talent — treating it as a projection double-counts the efficiency residual.
7. FIRST TEST AND VERDICT — Smallest test: `ep_load(2026, "weekly")`, build the WR xFP table through Week 8, and check (i) the leaderboard's top tier matches the chart's implied elite names qualitatively and (ii) FP−xFP gap correlates negatively with next-4-week FP change (the regression premise) on 2024–25 history. Verdict: **LEARN-ONLY** (method already known to the corpus; this chart's values stay unverified).

Actual TDs − xTD is the TD-luck residual: the companion post's framing ("players that should have scored MORE TDs") is the negative-residual tail — players whose opportunity profile says their TD count is due to rise. It extends the engine's existing expected-points work by naming the exact open implementation, its license, and its known bug — replacing any hand-rolled or unattributed xTD in the stack with a versioned, inspectable one. The corpus's anytime-TD reproduction lane (play-by-play 2012/2018/2024 pulls in the sweep archive) is the natural validation harness.

Koalaty's table values are image-locked/UNVERIFIED; the *method credit* (ffopportunity) is verified from his own companion post and the project's docs.

1. WHAT IT IS — A QB deep-ball accuracy leaderboard over Weeks 1–7 2026, built on Fantasy Points Data's hand charting. "Deep throw" in charting products is conventionally an attempt traveling 20+ air yards (the exact threshold is the vendor's and is not stated in the recovered text — UNVERIFIED). "Accuracy" here is charted accurate-throw rate — the share of deep attempts the charter grades as on-target/catchable (ball location), NOT raw completion percentage: a perfectly placed deep ball dropped or defended counts as accurate in charted systems, while a completed but poorly placed ball may not. That distinction is the chart's whole value: it separates QB ball placement from receiver/defender outcomes on the lowest-frequency, highest-variance throws. Volume context: QBs attempt roughly 3–6 deep balls a game, so a 7-week sample is ~25–40 attempts per QB.
2. WHERE TO FIND IT — Post: https://x.com/sfdata9ers/status/2116255819834749331 . Primary source: Fantasy Points Data Suite (hand-charted accuracy/ball-location data; the vendor documents QB throw-accuracy breakdowns by field location as a Data Suite feature) https://fantasypointsdata.com/ .
3. HOW TO USE IT — The true charted metric needs the vendor subscription. GSE's free approximations, in descending fidelity: (i) deep completion % and deep CPOE from nflverse PBP — filter `air_yards >= 20`, CPOE = actual completion % minus nflfastR model completion probability on those plays (completion probability is a PBP column) — outcome-based, not placement-based, must be labeled a proxy; (ii) FTN charting via nflverse adds `is_catchable_ball` on every target, so a deep *catchable-ball rate* (share of 20+ air-yard attempts charted catchable) is computable free and is much closer to the charted concept — this is the recommended GSE build, with drops (also FTN) separating QB placement from receiver finishing. Inputs: PBP + FTN charting, both in the free stack; ~48h FTN latency fits the weekly cycle.
4. WHERE TO USE IT — GSE prediction engine, **matchups layer** (QB deep placement × opposing defense's deep-ball defense and safety structure — the deep game is where scheme matchups produce explosive plays, and EPA is explosive-driven), with a **situations layer** cross-feed (trailing/game-script states force deep volume; accuracy under forced volume is the real trait). Paired with this batch's target-depth distribution dossier (2112686802566041882, same creator and vendor): depth *frequency* × depth *accuracy* = true deep threat, and the pair replaces any single "deep passer" rating in GSE's QB profiles. DFS/props: deep accuracy is the reasoning input for QB longest-completion and WR deep-target props.
5. CREATIVE ANGLES — (a) Accuracy–volume divergence as a trade/injury tell: a QB whose deep accuracy craters while volume holds is a classic hidden-injury (shoulder/elbow) pattern — wire the FTN-based deep catchable rate into the health layer as a monitoring stat, not a headline. (b) Weather interaction: deep accuracy is the first stat wind kills; a venues-weather-refs cross (deep-reliant QB + 15+ mph wind game) is a concrete, reasoning-visible totals/prop input. (c) Content OS: "most accurate deep throwers (charted, not completion %)" is an explainer-grade chart — it teaches the audience the placement-vs-outcome distinction that GSE's whole brand rests on.
6. RISKS AND LIMITS — Tiny samples: ~30 attempts means a 60% accurate rate has a confidence interval roughly ±18 points — ranks are tiers at best, and the chart (as recovered) shows no attempt counts or intervals. Threshold (20+ yards assumed) and accuracy rubric are the vendor's, unstated here. Charted accuracy is a judgment grade; Fantasy Points and PFF grades will differ on the same throws. All values UNVERIFIED / image-locked. Survivorship: QBs benched mid-window shrink the denominator unevenly.
7. FIRST TEST AND VERDICT — Smallest test: build the FTN-based deep catchable-ball rate (20+ air yards, Weeks 1–7 2026, min 20 attempts) from nflverse + FTN charting and compare its top/bottom tiers to the chart's implied ordering as far as transcription allows; also report deep CPOE beside it to show where outcome and placement disagree. Verdict: **LEARN-ONLY** (the FTN proxy build is the TEST candidate; the vendor chart itself stays a reference).

### #89 — @bsdyork — 2033272882873147662
Post: https://x.com/bsdyork/status/2033272882873147662
TEXT RECOVERY: FULL (long-form post recovered verbatim from `raw/2026-10-30-extra100/s12_playaction.json`, Grok x_search capture, 2026-03-15 20:03 UTC). Recovered text, in substance: "The College QB Metrics That Translate Best to the NFL — 1. Accuracy (Especially Intermediate Throws): completion %, CPOE, on-target rate; intermediate accuracy (10–19 yards) correlates most strongly with NFL performance, short second, deep least predictive; typical elite college threshold 65–70% completion. 2. Decision Making (Turnover Rate): TD/INT ratio, turnover-worthy plays, INT rate — one of the most stable indicators; processing speed carries over. 3. EPA per Play: elite college QBs 0.30+ EPA/play. 4. Throw Depth Profile: QBs throwing deep on <15% of attempts had much worse NFL success; 15%+ deep rate far more successful. 5. Pressure Performance: completion % under pressure, sack rate, pressure-to-sack conversion; ~35–40% of NFL dropbacks face pressure. 6. Time to Throw / Processing Speed: QBs holding the ball >3.2s regularly in college often struggle in the NFL. 7. 3rd Down Efficiency: 3rd-down conversion, 3rd-down EPA, 3rd & 7+ performance. 8. Sack Rate: elite <5%; bad translations 8–10%. Metrics that DON'T translate: wins/losses (team-driven), red-zone stats (volatile), raw yardage (scheme-dependent), 40-yard dash. What teams track: composite EPA + CPOE. Boiled down to 5: (1) intermediate accuracy, (2) decision making, (3) pressure performance, (4) processing speed, (5) EPA efficiency — 'those five cover ~80% of predictive signal.'"
1. WHAT IT IS — A college-to-NFL QB translation framework, not a single metric. Its components, defined precisely: **CPOE** = actual completion % minus expected completion % from a model conditioning on throw depth, receiver openness/separation, throw width, pressure, down, distance, field position and clock (PFF's college CPOE explainer, below, gives this construction). **Intermediate accuracy** = completion/on-target rate on throws targeted 10–19 yards downfield. **EPA/play** = mean Expected Points Added per QB play, where EPA is the change in expected points (from down/distance/field-position state) caused by the play. **Pressure-to-sack rate** = sacks taken ÷ dropbacks pressured. **Sack rate** = sacks ÷ dropbacks. **Deep rate** = share of attempts targeted 20+ yards (the post's "<15% / 15%+" split; exact deep cutoff unstated in the post — UNVERIFIED). The post's quantitative claims (65–70% elite completion threshold, 0.30+ EPA/play elite, <5% / 8–10% sack-rate bands, >3.2s time-to-throw warning, "~80% of predictive signal") are asserted without a cited study, sample, or correlation coefficient in the post itself — creator claims, UNVERIFIED as stated. Independent check found in verification (below) materially weakens the headline: the closest matching public study reports intermediate accuracy explaining only R² ≈ 0.038 of NFL EPA/play variance — directionally supportive, nowhere near "most predictive" in an absolute sense.
2. WHERE TO FIND IT — Post: https://x.com/bsdyork/status/2033272882873147662 . Primary-source / verification URLs: the matching public study of exactly these variables (TWP rate, big-time-throw rate, accuracy overall and on 10–19-yard throws, pressure-to-sack rate vs NFL EPA/play, QBs with ≥150 snaps since 2015) — https://analyticfootball.com/the-best-single-college-statistic-to-predict-nfl-qb-performance/ ; PFF's college CPOE construction and college→NFL stability check — https://www.pff.com/news/college-football-what-college-completion-percentage-over-expected-cpoe-tells-us-about-the-2022-nfl-draft-qb-class . Underlying data sources: PFF college charting (on-target %, turnover-worthy plays — paywalled), Sports Info Solutions, and the free cfbfastR / cfbfastR-data play-by-play (EPA and completion data for FBS; no openness charting, so true CPOE must be modeled, not read off).
3. HOW TO USE IT — Needs, per component: (a) intermediate accuracy and EPA/play — computable from cfbfastR play-by-play (target depth is derivable from air-yards/pass-location fields where present; coverage of depth fields varies by season/provider, so validate per season before trusting a split). (b) CPOE — requires an expected-completion model; GSE can fit one on cfbfastR features (depth, down, distance, field position, pressure proxy) but it will NOT be PFF's CPOE and must never be labeled as such. (c) Turnover-worthy play rate and on-target rate — charting products (PFF/SIS), not legally free; approximate with INT rate + fumble rate and label the approximation. (d) Pressure splits and time-to-throw — not in free college pbp at NFL fidelity; treat as cite-only from charting vendors. A defensible GSE build is therefore a 3-of-5 version (intermediate accuracy, INT/turnover rate, EPA/play, plus sack rate) with the other two slots explicitly marked "charting-only, not computed."
4. WHERE TO USE IT — GSE prediction engine, **everything-else layer (player evaluation / priors), feeding the usage and matchups layers at draft/rookie scope**: this is not a weekly in-season signal for established starters; its real job is (i) setting priors for rookie/first-year QBs and backup-to-starter transitions, where the weekly engine otherwise has no NFL sample, and (ii) the offseason/draft content cycle. It extends — does not replace — the corpus's existing NFL-side QB metrics (EPA+CPOE composites already used in-season). Second home: research corpus, as a pre-registered claim set to test (see §7).
5. CREATIVE ANGLES — (a) Flip it: apply the same five traits to *college defenses'* future NFL translators later — the framework generalizes to a "what translates" series per position, a full draft-season content franchise. (b) Combine with the pressure-split dossier in this batch (@sfdata9ers, Fantasy Points Data): college pressure performance is the prospect-side version of the NFL pressured-vs-clean EPA cliff — a QB whose college pressure numbers were poor AND whose early NFL pressured EPA is bottom-tier is a two-source fade, stronger than either alone. (c) The negative list (wins, raw yardage, red-zone stats, 40 time) is itself publishable reasoning: it tells readers exactly what GSE's draft grades ignore and why. (d) For DFS packs: rookie-QB pricing in early-season slates is where a translation prior has direct money value, because salary algorithms lag role/performance news.
6. RISKS AND LIMITS — The post overstates certainty: no sample sizes, no correlations, no study cited; the one matching public study found R² values of 0.006–0.038 for its headline variables (i.e., each explains under 4% of variance), found pressure-to-sack on non-blitz plays and turnover-worthy-play rate on non-blitz plays at least as informative, and explicitly warned these are weak predictors individually. Survivorship/selection bias: college QBs who reach the NFL are pre-filtered by draft capital, which itself encodes these stats. Scheme confounding: college completion % is heavily offense-driven (the post concedes this for yardage but not for its own accuracy thresholds). Thresholds (65–70%, 0.30 EPA, 3.2s) are round-number heuristics, UNVERIFIED. Era drift: college spread/RPO prevalence changes what "intermediate accuracy" samples look like across classes.
7. FIRST TEST AND VERDICT — Smallest real test: pull cfbfastR play-by-play for the last five draft classes, compute intermediate completion %, INT rate, EPA/play and sack rate for drafted QBs, and regress/ rank-correlate against their NFL EPA/play over their first 500 dropbacks; compare the rank correlation to draft position alone. If the composite does not beat draft capital, it stays a content framework only. **Verdict: LEARN-ONLY** (framework cataloged in full; the "~80% of signal" claim is recorded as UNVERIFIED and contradicted in strength — though not direction — by the closest public study).

Post: https://x.com/sfdata9ers/status/2116309674526183481
TEXT RECOVERY: PARTIAL (post text recovered in full from `raw/2026-10-30-pm/sfdata9ers.txt` — "QB EPA when Pressured vs. Kept Clean — Data: @FantasyPtsData, min. 105 dropbacks", Fri 30 Oct 2026 23:22 GMT — but this capture's chart image is marked IMAGE VALUES UNREADABLE. Chart values below are CITED from the identical chart's re-posting, 2116338607163509087, read in-browser in the 2026-10-30 PM report; they are the same chart's values, not a separate reading of this image.)
1. WHAT IT IS — A full-league QB split chart: for every QB with ≥105 dropbacks (Weeks 1–7, 2026), **EPA per dropback when pressured** vs **EPA per dropback when kept clean**. EPA/dropback = mean Expected Points Added across a QB's dropbacks (pass attempts + sacks + scrambles as charted), split by whether the dropback was pressured (a defender charted as affecting the QB) or clean. Values cited from the identical sibling chart: pressured — Justin Herbert +0.06 and Jared Goff +0.04 the only positives, Drake Maye exactly 0.00, bottom Justin Fields −0.76 and Jake Browning −0.78; kept clean — Maye +0.61, Jordan Love +0.56, Dak Prescott and Matthew Stafford +0.54, Jaxson Dart +0.51, bottom Browning +0.10, Cooper Rush +0.03, Fields −0.03. This posting is the original of that chart; 2116338607163509087 is its re-post and 2116333512831684724 its self-RT (next dossier).
2. WHERE TO FIND IT — Post: https://x.com/sfdata9ers/status/2116309674526183481 . Primary source (the charting vendor named in the post): Fantasy Points Data — https://fantasypointsdata.com/whats-new (its Data Suite documents the Pressured vs Clean QB split as a first-class table: "QB-side … EPA/DB and FP/DB side-by-side for Man vs Zone, … Blitz, Pressured vs Clean, Play Action, and Motion"). Sibling chart with values read: https://x.com/sfdata9ers/status/2116338607163509087 .
3. HOW TO USE IT — The split is approximable in-house: nflverse play-by-play (via nflfastR) carries EPA per play and QB hit/pressure-adjacent fields, but true pressure charting is the vendor's human/derived product — nflverse alone does not carry a play-level "pressured" flag at Fantasy Points Data fidelity. Practical route: (i) cite the vendor table for publication, or (ii) build an approximation from nflverse sack/QB-hit flags + Next Gen Stats pressure data where legally available, and label it an approximation with a different name. Inputs needed: dropbacks ≥ a stated minimum (the post uses 105), a pressure indicator per dropback, EPA per dropback. Definition risk lives entirely in the pressure indicator.
4. WHERE TO USE IT — GSE prediction engine, **matchups layer**: this is the QB half of "pass rush vs pocket." A defense's pressure rate is only worth what the specific opposing QB loses under pressure — the clean-minus-pressured **cliff** (e.g., Maye 0.61 of cliff from an elite base vs Fields 0.73 of cliff from a negative base — collapse vs chaos, two different failure modes) is the publishable, priceable number. It replaces any generic "QB struggles under pressure" reasoning line with a cited, quantified split, and it pairs directly with each defense's pressure rate already tracked in the corpus's pressure dossiers.
5. CREATIVE ANGLES — (a) Cliff-as-trait: track the cliff week-over-week as a QB stability metric; a narrowing cliff is a development signal content can narrate before box scores show it. (b) Three-number matchup line for the 360° card: defense pressure rate × QB pressured EPA × QB clean EPA — one line that prices the whole pass-rush matchup. (c) DFS angle: QBs with elite clean EPA facing low-pressure defenses are ceiling plays whose salaries are set on blended EPA. (d) Combine with the behind-the-line-of-scrimmage rate dossier already in the corpus: Herbert throws behind the LOS least in the league AND is the best QB under pressure (+0.06) — a coherent "downfield under fire" identity no single chart shows.
6. RISKS AND LIMITS — Pressure charting is vendor-subjective; definitions differ across Fantasy Points Data, PFF, SIS and NGS, so cross-vendor comparisons are invalid. ≥105 dropbacks is still only ~2–7 games per QB early-season; pressured EPA is high-variance and confounds QB, offensive line, and scheme (quick-game offenses manufacture "clean" pockets; a QB who throws in 2.2s is rarely charted pressured). Independent corroboration of instability exists in the verification pass: NBC's Week 5 takeaways note a QB's pressured stats flipping from great (regular season) to near-worst (postseason) — pressured EPA specifically is the flaky half of this split; clean-pocket EPA is the more stable half. This specific image was unreadable in the raw capture (values via the identical sibling chart — see recovery note).
7. FIRST TEST AND VERDICT — As set in the PM report and still the right test: rebuild the split from GSE's warehoused play-by-play for Weeks 1–7 under an explicitly documented pressure proxy and check whether the extremes hold (Herbert/Goff positive under pressure; Fields/Browning at the bottom; Maye top clean). If the ordering survives the proxy, adopt as a weekly matchups-layer table with the proxy named on the card. **Verdict: TEST.**

### #92 — @run_the_sims — 2115536627305251185
Post: https://x.com/run_the_sims/status/2115536627305251185
TEXT RECOVERY: PARTIAL (recovered text from `raw/2026-10-30-pm/run_the_sims.txt`: "RT @run_the_sims: Luck + Penalties vs Wins Above Expected by point differential, 2026 Weeks 1-7 — Point differential (record): 1. 49ers +58…" — truncated at the timeline fold, exactly as in the original chart post's capture; the chart's luck/penalty axes were not transcribed at timeline size. The construction spec below is recovered in full from the author's own companion posts already dossiered in the corpus, MIDDAY-X100 M-62/M-63.)
1. WHAT IT IS — A self-RT of the author's "Luck + Penalties vs Wins Above Expected" chart (original chart post: 2115426530104836314, dossiered as ON3 B8). The construction, from the author's own method posts: for each team through Week 7, compute (a) **Penalties** = cumulative win-probability (WP) swing from judgment-call penalties — holding, defensive pass interference, illegal contact, roughing — for and against; (b) **Luck** = cumulative WP swing from dropped passes, dropped interceptions, field-goal/extra-point outcomes, and fumble recoveries; (c) compare the sum against **Wins Above Expected** = actual wins minus expected wins derived from point differential. WP swing per play = WP after the play minus WP before it, under a win-probability model on down/distance/field position/score/clock. Point-differential anchor list as captured from the original: SF +58 (6-1), MIN +51 (6-0), JAX +50 (4-2), SEA +43 (4-3), CIN +41 (4-2), KC +40 (5-1), CHI +37 (5-2), BUF +26 (5-1), DET… (rest below the fold, unread — UNVERIFIED / fold-locked). A stated assumption (companion post): subjective judgment calls are counted inside luck/penalties on the argument that their distribution is effectively random across teams. Data credit on the chart: @DataWithBliss.
2. WHERE TO FIND IT — Post: https://x.com/run_the_sims/status/2115536627305251185 . Original chart: https://x.com/run_the_sims/status/2115426530104836314 . Method spec post: https://x.com/run_the_sims/status/2115426532344651938 . Author's site: runthesims.com (named in the author's later corpus posts; the chart itself is an X artifact). Upstream data credit: @DataWithBliss (the luck-play accounting behind the construction, dossiered in EXTRA100 A5).
3. HOW TO USE IT — Fully reproducible in principle from data GSE already has: nflverse play-by-play carries penalty type/acceptance, fumbles and recoveries, drops are NOT in standard pbp (dropped-pass and dropped-INT components need charting or a proxy — that is the one non-free input), plus a WP model (nflfastR's built-in WP or GSE's own). Build: tag each play in the four penalty families and the four luck families, sum WP deltas for/against per team, regress or tabulate against Pythagorean/point-differential expected wins. The drop components are the fidelity bottleneck — either license charting or publish the construction minus drops, explicitly renamed.
4. WHERE TO USE IT — GSE prediction engine, **everything-else layer (team-quality adjustment) and the postgame grading / regression lane — explicitly NOT play formation**: per the corpus's standing doctrine on all luck accounting, luck explains results, it never forms plays; its engine job is to discount banked records (a 6-0 team whose differential and luck ledger say 4-2 is a different matchup input than a true 6-0) before the matchups layer prices them. It extends the corpus's two other luck constructions (nfl_simulator's Deserve-To-Win and DataWithBliss's dashboard) — three independent constructions of the same concept is a methods-comparison asset, and their disagreements are themselves information.
5. CREATIVE ANGLES — (a) "Record vs reality" is a weekly publishable reasoning line: the luck ledger is how GSE explains, with arithmetic shown, why it is fading a winning team or backing a losing one — the exact anti-market-echo reasoning the doctrine demands. (b) Penalty-luck specifically has a coaching angle: judgment-call WP swing for/against is a cleaner "discipline" number than raw penalty yards, and pairing it with crew assignments (venues-weather-refs layer) is a second-order matchup input nobody publishes. (c) Combine with the playoff-leverage work in the corpus: luck-inflated teams late in the season are leverage-game fades with a story attached.
6. RISKS AND LIMITS — WP-model dependence: every component inherits the chosen WP model's errors, and two builders' luck numbers will differ by model alone. The judgment-call-as-luck assumption is stated but contestable (some teams systematically draw/commit more DPIs by scheme — man-heavy defenses commit more contact fouls by design, which is skill/scheme, not luck). Drops need charting GSE doesn't have for free. Seven weeks is a small sample for WP-swing sums dominated by a handful of huge plays (one dropped TD can be +36% WP — see EXTRA100 A5's GB example). This RT adds no data beyond the original; double-counting risk as with all self-RTs.
7. FIRST TEST AND VERDICT — Smallest test: rebuild the penalties half only (fully in pbp, no charting needed) for Weeks 1–7 2026 from the warehouse WP model and check the known qualitative result from the author's related post — Green Bay last in penalty luck through 7 weeks (corroborated independently in the verification pass: a RawChili/Jaguars article quoting the author's Oct 26 post, "The Packers rank last in penalty 'luck' through 7 weeks"). If GB-last reproduces, the pipeline is sound and the drops half becomes the only open input. **Verdict: LEARN-ONLY** (construction captured in full and reproducible; grading-lane use only, per standing luck doctrine).

Post: https://x.com/StatsHubCom/status/2116531439302742472
TEXT RECOVERY: FULL (recovered verbatim from `raw/2026-10-31-am/StatsHubCom.txt`: "Good recent trend for Williams and some other books like Unibet offering some bigger odds too if you've got an account with the lesser used operators.", Sat 31 Oct 2026 14:03 GMT; no image/chart. Which "Williams" and which market are not specified in the text — UNVERIFIED referent; in context of the account's soccer-tips window it is a player-prop odds note, but the dossier does not guess further.)
1. WHAT IT IS — A price-dispersion note: a player ("Williams") is on a "good recent trend" (a hit-rate streak on some prop, market unspecified), and the post's actual claim is about the market — smaller/less-used bookmakers (Unibet named) are offering bigger odds on it than the headline books. The mechanism underneath: prop prices are not uniform across books; recreational books with thinner prop markets shade prices less efficiently, so the same selection can be materially better priced off the main screen.
2. WHERE TO FIND IT — Post: https://x.com/StatsHubCom/status/2116531439302742472 . Vendor primary source: https://www.statshub.com/ (its Odds API — 400+ bookmakers in one feed — is the industrial version of exactly this comparison, verified on the homepage in this pass).
3. HOW TO USE IT — As a procedure: for any prop GSE has already selected on matchup grounds, pull the price from the widest available book set before settling — including the less-used operators — because prop-market dispersion is widest exactly there. GSE's existing price-check step (The Odds API, on account) already aggregates many books; the post's marginal point is to not stop at the two headline US books when a line looks short.
4. WHERE TO USE IT — GSE prediction engine, **market-last layer** (price-check only; it can improve the price of a play that already exists, it can never create one — the post's own framing, trend-first, is the reverse of GSE doctrine and is not adopted). Also GSE Content OS as a reader-education point: "the same bet costs different amounts at different shops" is honest, useful, pick-free content.
5. CREATIVE ANGLES — (a) A standing "price dispersion watch" — logging how far the best and worst prices on GSE's own published categories diverge each week — is publishable market literacy that gives away no pick. (b) For the DFS/props product, dispersion data over a season identifies which books are systematically soft on which prop families, a durable edge inventory for the market-last layer.
6. RISKS AND LIMITS — Referent and market unspecified (UNVERIFIED); "good recent trend" is an unquantified streak claim — small-sample hit-rate streaks are the weakest prop evidence there is. Lesser-used books carry counterparty, limits, and withdrawal frictions the post doesn't mention; a bigger price at a book that limits winners quickly is worth less than it prints.
7. FIRST TEST AND VERDICT — No standalone test; the practice (widest-set price check) is already GSE doctrine via The Odds API — this post adds only the lesser-operator emphasis, filed as a reminder inside the market-last checklist. **Verdict: CONTEXT-ONLY.**

### #100 — @StatsHubCom — 2116522480030588949
Post: https://x.com/StatsHubCom/status/2116522480030588949
TEXT RECOVERY: FULL for text (recovered from `raw/2026-10-31-am/StatsHubCom.txt`: "RT @KP_tipster: #SerieA Play: (3u, 0.5u, 0.1u) Cambiaghi + Ferguson + Ondoa FW @ 2.33, 8.79, 44.19🪜 ✍️ All data via @StatsHubCom", Sat 31 Oct 2026 13:27 GMT. "FW" is the tipster's notation as captured; the ladder structure is clear from the numbers. Cambiaghi, Ferguson and Ondoa are Serie A players named by the tipster; the prop market (likely an escalating shots/goals ladder) is not spelled out in the text — market UNVERIFIED.)
1. WHAT IT IS — A retweet of @KP_tipster's Serie A "ladder" bet: three escalating legs on three named players staked 3u / 0.5u / 0.1u at decimal odds 2.33 / 8.79 / 44.19 respectively. The ladder structure is the content: bet the probable version big, the medium version small, the longshot version tiny — a stake-weighted probability staircase on correlated escalation of the same underlying prop idea. It is a staking construction, not a prediction model; no hit rates or data are shown, and the post closes with the data credit "All data via @StatsHubCom."
2. WHERE TO FIND IT — Post: https://x.com/StatsHubCom/status/2116522480030588949 . Vendor primary source: https://www.statshub.com/ (the credited data source; Player Trends — "the strongest player streaks on every prop, over 3 to 40 games" — is the tool this workflow runs on, verified on the homepage in this pass).
3. HOW TO USE IT — The ladder is directly describable in GSE's terms: for a prop family with nested outcomes (e.g., a receiver at 40+ / 70+ / 100+ yards), allocate stake in decreasing proportion to decreasing probability, sized so the total exposure equals the single-play stake the matchup grade earned. GSE's props/SGP packaging can express this today; nothing external is needed beyond the discipline of pre-committing the three stake fractions (3 / 0.5 / 0.1 is this tipster's ratio — steep; a flatter 2 / 1 / 0.5 expresses the same idea with less longshot drag).
4. WHERE TO USE IT — GSE product/packaging layer (paid props & SGP packs) and the **market-last layer** for stake expression — the ladder is a way to *express* a graded read at three prices, never a way to form one. It extends GSE's existing unit staking with a nested-outcome variant for the specific case where the same player prop exists at multiple thresholds on the same book.
5. CREATIVE ANGLES — (a) Ladder posts are inherently good content shapes (three rungs, three prices, one idea) — GSE's version publishes the *structure and the reasoning* in the paid pack while the public post teases only the category, per the paywall rule. (b) Backtesting the ladder vs flat staking on GSE's own graded prop history is a clean, honest research note: does the staircase actually beat flat units on nested props, or is it entertainment? That is a publishable methods answer either way. (c) The "All data via @StatsHubCom" credit line, again: third-party tipsters advertising the data source for free is the distribution mechanic worth copying for GSE's own free tables.
6. RISKS AND LIMITS — Nested/ladder legs on the same player are positively correlated in outcome but each rung is priced with its own margin; the 44.19 rung at 0.1u is mostly lottery decoration. The tipster's market, hit rates and record are unstated — the selections are UNVERIFIED tips, and the ladder's elegance says nothing about whether the underlying read was good. Escalation structures can disguise total exposure (3.6u across the ladder vs a "0.1u longshot" headline).
7. FIRST TEST AND VERDICT — Smallest test, if ever run: replay the ladder vs flat staking over one season of GSE's already-graded nested props (receptions/yards thresholds) and compare ROI and drawdown — a spreadsheet exercise on existing records, no new data. Not scheduled; filed as a packaging option. **Verdict: CONTEXT-ONLY** (staking pattern cataloged with its math; the tip itself is not adopted or endorsed).
